Direct report, screenshotted: "looks like no styling at all" — content and layout structure were all present (table columns, image sizes, button shapes), but every color, font, and spacing value was missing, leaving the browser's bare defaults (serif headings, flat grey buttons) everywhere a `var(--wp--…)` custom property was supposed to resolve.

Root cause: `YeffoPrint_Admin_Token_Bridge::inline_css()` hand-parsed `wp_get_global_settings()`'s merged-settings array — a plausible-looking mismatch between the assumed and actual shape of that array meant it silently produced an empty string, so `wp_add_inline_style()` never actually defined a single `--wp--…` custom property anywhere on the page. Nothing errored; every rule in `app-shell.css`/`records.css`/`global.css` that referenced a token just quietly fell back to nothing, while this app's own *static* CSS (fixed pixel widths, `display:flex`, `border-radius`) kept working fine since none of that depends on a custom property resolving — exactly the mix of "some structure, zero brand styling" the screenshot showed.

Fixed by no longer hand-deriving this output at all: `inline_css()` now returns `wp_get_global_stylesheet( [ 'variables' ] )` directly — the exact core function WordPress itself calls to produce the `<style id="global-styles-inline-css">` block every block-theme page already gets on the front end. Using WordPress's own tested generator instead of re-implementing its settings-merging logic by hand removes this entire class of bug, whatever the precise mismatch was.

## Custom admin dashboard, Phase 3: Sticker Sizes, Web Design Packages, Maintenance Subscribers

Direct request: "Keep going!" Same WP-core-REST pattern as Phase 2, rounding out the rest of the "simple CRUD" screens from the plan before Phase 4's two genuinely hard ones (Pricing Rules' repeaters, Templates' drag-position editor).

- **Sticker Sizes** (`views/sticker-sizes.js`) adds the one real validation rule in this batch: exactly one tier may be the "custom size" tier (customer types exact dimensions; price comes from a $/sq in rate instead of a fixed price — `YeffoPrint_Sticker_Size_Meta`'s own docblock). That used to be enforced only inside `class-sticker-size-editor.php`'s classic `save()` — fine while that editor was the only way to write the field, but this screen writes straight through WP core's `/wp/v2/yp_sticker_size` route instead, which has no way to know about an editor-specific rule. Moved the enforcement down into the data layer itself: `YeffoPrint_Sticker_Size_Meta::enforce_single_custom_tier()`, hooked on `added_post_meta`/`updated_post_meta` for the `_yp_is_custom_size` key, un-marks every other tier the moment any one is marked custom — regardless of which UI (or WP-CLI, or anything else) did the writing. The classic editor's own copy of this same check is left as-is; having both is redundant, not conflicting.
- **Web Design Packages** (`views/web-design-packages.js`) needed one small data-layer change first: `_yp_features` (the bullet-point list) was deliberately *not* REST-registered when it was first built, since only the classic editor ever touched it. Registered it now (`class-web-design-package-meta.php`, `type => array, items => string`) — the one field in this whole phase that didn't already have `show_in_rest` on.
- **Maintenance Subscribers** (`views/maintenance.js`) is the one read-only screen in the plan — these records are written entirely by the Stripe webhook, and there's no classic editor for this CPT at all to replace. List + a read-only detail drawer (plan, status, renewal date, linked WordPress account if any, Stripe subscription/customer IDs) via the same `GET /wp/v2/yp_maintenance_sub`, no write path exposed anywhere in this screen.
- Added `.yp-pill--warn`/`.yp-pill--crit` to `records.css` (Maintenance's `past_due`/hypothetical error states) alongside the existing `--good`/`--neutral` — the first screen needing more than a simple active/draft binary, and the same two-state palette every future status-pipeline screen (Custom Orders next) will need too.

Verify: mark a Sticker Size tier "custom" and confirm whichever tier was previously custom automatically un-marks; add a Web Design Package with a few feature bullets and confirm they render correctly on the live Web Design page's pricing table; open Maintenance Subscribers and confirm the list and a subscriber's detail drawer match what's in the Stripe Dashboard.

## Custom admin dashboard, Phase 4a: Pricing Rules (the first genuinely new admin REST endpoint)

Direct request: "Keep going!" Pricing Rules is the first screen in this plan that couldn't piggyback on WP core's own `/wp/v2/{type}` routes at all — `yp_pricing_rule` has no REST-registered meta whatsoever (base price, design fee, and every tier/adjustment map are plain `get_post_meta()`/JSON-encoded arrays, by design — see `YeffoPrint_Pricing_Rule`/`YeffoPrint_Sticker_Pricing`'s own docblocks), so there was nothing for core REST to expose even in principle.

- **`YeffoPrint_Admin_Pricing_Controller`** (`includes/rest/admin/class-admin-pricing-controller.php`) — `GET`/`POST /admin/pricing-rule`, gated by the same `YeffoPrint_Rest_Security::admin_write()` every admin route uses. Deliberately a thin wrapper, not a reimplementation: `save_rule()` calls the exact same `YeffoPrint_Pricing_Rule::save()`/`YeffoPrint_Sticker_Pricing::save()` the classic editor's `save_post_yp_pricing_rule` hook already calls (`class-pricing-rule-editor.php`) — same validation, same tier-resolution logic, same single-active-record model, just reached over REST instead of a form POST. The response also carries `tier_types`/`sticker.types`/`sticker.shapes` (the same label maps the classic editor's PHP constants define) on every response, so the client never hardcodes a second copy of those.
- **`views/pricing.js`** edits "the one active record" directly on the page — no list, no drawer, no id in the URL, since there's only ever one thing to edit. Four panels: Base Pricing, Bulk Discount Tiers, Sticker Pricing (rate + type/shape adjustment grids, built dynamically from the server's `types`/`shapes` maps rather than a second hardcoded key list), and Sticker Bulk Discount Tiers. The two tier repeaters are the first genuinely new interactive component in this app: rows are read straight from their table's live DOM inputs at save time rather than mirrored into a parallel JS array kept in sync on every keystroke — add/remove only ever appends or removes a `<tr>`, so there's nothing that can drift between what's on screen and what gets saved.
- New `.yp-panel`/`.yp-tier-table` styles in `records.css` — the first "settings-style" (single-record, no list) screen and the first editable-repeater-table component, both reusable by later phases.

Verify: change the base price or add a bulk-discount tier, save, and confirm the public `/pricing/calculate` endpoint and the live label configurator reflect it immediately; same for a Sticker Pricing change against `/pricing/calculate-sticker` and the Custom Stickers form.

## Custom admin dashboard, Phase 5: Templates and Field Presets (the hardest screen in the plan)

Direct request: "Go ahead and start the next phase." `yp_template` and `yp_field_preset` are both already reachable through WP core's own `/wp/v2/{type}` routes — every simple field (title, content, status, featured image, and the flat meta: featured/popularity/vial_mockup/badge/preview_font) has always had `show_in_rest` on. The real gap, same shape as Phase 4a's Pricing Rules, is the handful of fields that were deliberately *never* REST-registered because only a classic editor ever needed to read them: `compatible_sizes`/`compatible_materials` (plain arrays, `class-template-meta.php`'s own docblock) and `field_schema` (its own class, `YeffoPrint_Field_Schema`, no meta registration of its own either — used identically by both post types).

- **Two new thin admin REST controllers** — `YeffoPrint_Admin_Template_Controller` (`GET`/`POST /admin/template/{id}`) and `YeffoPrint_Admin_Field_Preset_Controller` (`GET`/`POST /admin/field-preset/{id}`), each wrapping only the fields core REST can't reach: the Template controller returns/saves `compatible_sizes`/`compatible_materials`/`field_schema`; the Field Preset controller, having no artwork or compatibility concept at all, only ever touches `field_schema`. Both call `YeffoPrint_Field_Schema::update()` directly — the exact same sanitizer the classic editors' `save_post_{type}` hooks already call — never reimplementing it. Saving a Template or Field Preset from the new app is therefore always two sequential REST calls: core's own route first (so a brand-new record gets an id), then one of these for the rest. Nothing is written to the database until Save is actually clicked — unlike classic wp-admin's auto-draft, opening the "Add Template" drawer creates nothing on its own.
- **Where the field-type/alignment/formatting/badge label maps live is different from Pricing Rules' `tier_types`.** Pricing Rules' constants could ride along on the REST response because that screen only ever edits an *existing* record. A brand-new Template's "Add" drawer has to render its full field-schema editor and Badge dropdown before any post/id exists yet — before there's anything to `GET` from either new controller — so `YeffoPrint_Admin_App::enqueue_assets()` now localizes those constants once for the whole app instead (`yeffoprintAdminApp.fieldSchema`/`.badges`/`.previewFontSuggestions`), the same static-constants-via-`wp_localize_script` pattern `class-template-editor.php` already used for the classic version of this exact screen.
- **`assets/admin-app/field-schema-editor.js`** — the drag-to-position "Customization Fields" repeater, factored out of the classic `assets/admin/field-schema.js` into a standalone `YP.createFieldSchemaEditor(config)` widget so both `views/templates.js` and `views/field-presets.js` can share one implementation instead of each screen reimplementing the drag/keyboard-nudge/repeater logic. The biggest behavioral difference from the classic version: state used to sync to a hidden form input that submitted with a normal WP post-edit form; here it just lives in memory and the caller reads it via `getFields()` at REST-save time. `previewImageUrl` is optional — passing `''` (Field Presets, which has no artwork of its own — `class-field-preset-editor.php`'s own docblock) falls back to the same "no preview yet" empty state a Template without a featured image would also show. `setPreviewImage()` lets `views/templates.js` keep the drag preview in sync when the artwork picker changes, without this generic widget needing to know that concern exists.
- **`views/templates.js`** — the most involved screen so far: name/description, Active toggle, Featured/Popularity, Preview font (free text with a datalist of suggestions, exactly as free-form as the classic version), Badge, two `wp.media()` pickers (artwork/featured image, vial mockup), two checklists (Compatible Sizes/Materials, built from `GET /wp/v2/yp_size`/`yp_material`), and the field-schema editor. "Insert from preset" fetches every published Field Preset's `field_schema` up front (one `/admin/field-preset/{id}` call per preset — the same "no reason to expect more than a handful" assumption `YeffoPrint_Field_Schema::get_presets()`'s own docblock already makes) so the dropdown works the same as the classic editor's. One bug caught before shipping: an early draft created the field-schema editor twice per drawer open — once immediately as a loading placeholder, again once the checklists/presets/gap-fields finished loading — which would have silently doubled up the editor's `document`-level `mousemove`/`touchmove` drag listeners every single time an admin reopened the drawer. Fixed by only ever constructing the widget once, after all of that data has actually arrived (the Save button stays disabled until then; an artwork pick made in the meantime is just remembered and handed to the widget when it's finally built).
- **`views/field-presets.js`** — the same list/drawer/save shape, far simpler: name, Active toggle, and the field-schema editor with no artwork at all. `YP.bindMediaPicker()` (`app.js`) gained optional `onSelect`/`onRemove` callbacks to support the artwork-preview-sync behavior above, without changing anything for its two existing Phase-2 callers (Materials' swatch/hover pickers), which simply don't pass them.
- New `.yp-drawer--wide` modifier (`field-schema.css`) widens just the Templates/Field Presets drawer (880px vs. the default 420px) — everywhere else keeps the narrower drawer, since only these two screens have this much in one form. Also new: `.yp-admin-checklist` (the compatible-sizes/materials checkbox list) and the field-schema repeater's own row/grid/marker styles, split into their own `field-schema.css` rather than growing `records.css` further, since nothing else in the app uses them.

Verify: add a Template with artwork, a few customization fields (drag-positioned against the real artwork, and via the numeric inputs), Compatible Sizes/Materials, and confirm the live configurator at `/shop-labels/{template}/` renders the same fields in the same positions; build a Field Preset, insert it into a Template via "Insert Preset," and confirm its fields land with the preset's own settings (default position, since position is always set per-Template afterward).

## Custom admin dashboard, Phase 6: Custom Orders, Proofs, and a real Dashboard home

Direct request: "go ahead and" (the next phase, per the approved plan's own ordering). `yp_custom_order` and `yp_proof` both have `show_in_rest` on at the post-type level — every CPT here does — but unlike every earlier screen, *none* of either one's meta was ever REST-registered: `class-custom-order-editor.php`/`class-proof-editor.php` read and write every field with plain `get_post_meta()`/`update_post_meta()` directly, so there was nothing for WP core's own `/wp/v2/{type}` routes to expose at all. This is the first screen in the plan that's a full read/write surface end to end, not a small gap-filler on top of core REST.

- **`YeffoPrint_Proof_Meta::attach_file()`** — before writing any new REST code, the status-advance-to-"Awaiting Proof Approval" + customer-email logic moved down from `class-proof-editor.php` (previously two private methods only its own `save_post_yp_proof` hook could reach) into the data layer, same "business logic lives below any one UI" reasoning as `YeffoPrint_Sticker_Size_Meta::enforce_single_custom_tier()` (Phase 3). Both the classic editor's `save()` and the new admin app's proof-upload endpoint now call this one method, so a proof attached from either UI behaves identically.
- **Three new admin REST controllers**, all thin wrappers over existing data/business logic rather than reimplementations:
  - `YeffoPrint_Admin_Custom_Order_Controller` (`GET /admin/custom-orders`, `GET`/`POST /admin/custom-order/{id}`) — read-only past `status`, mirroring the classic editor exactly (everything else is what the customer submitted or payment completion filled in; neither UI lets staff edit those). The detail payload branches on order type the same way `class-custom-order-editor.php::render_details_box()` already does, assembling the full Fully-Custom-Design or Custom-Stickers field set plus resolved upload URLs, batch rows, and the embedded proofs list.
  - `YeffoPrint_Admin_Proof_Controller` (`GET /admin/proofs`, `POST /admin/proof`, `DELETE /admin/proof/{id}`) — `create_proof()` does nothing the classic editor's `save()` doesn't already do: create the post, then hand off to `YeffoPrint_Proof_Meta::attach_file()`.
  - `YeffoPrint_Admin_Dashboard_Controller` (`GET /admin/dashboard-summary`) — the same four queries `YeffoPrint_Dashboard_Widgets` already runs for the classic reskin's Dashboard page (`wc_get_orders( status: 'processing' )`, the two `_yp_status` meta queries, `YeffoPrint_Maintenance_Sub_Meta::get_active()`), reached over REST instead of rendered server-side. Reuses `YeffoPrint_Dashboard_Widgets::due_date_days()` directly rather than a second copy of that option-reading logic — its require moved out of the `is_admin()`-only block into the unconditional section, since a REST request to this endpoint isn't an `is_admin()` context but still needs that class defined.
- **Router gained one real feature**: `#/{section}/{subId}` (`app.js`'s `currentSection()`, previously an exact-match `currentSectionId()`). The Dashboard's Pending Proofs/Awaiting Approval rows link straight into that specific order's own detail (`#/orders/123`) instead of only ever landing on the bare Orders list — `views/orders.js`'s `load()` accepts an optional order id and auto-opens its detail drawer once the list has loaded.
- **`views/orders.js`** — list (search + status filter, the filter's own option labels localized once via `yeffoprintAdminApp.customOrderStatuses` for the same "list needs it before any detail response could carry it" reason Phase 5's `fieldSchema`/`badges` were localized) and a detail drawer: every submitted field, an editable Status `<select>` (disabled/hidden entirely for an unpaid order, same as the classic Status box), the proofs list, an inline "+ Add Proof" (`wp.media()` picker straight to `POST /admin/proof`, no separate order-picker needed since the drawer already knows which order it's for), and the copyable customer approval link.
- **`views/proofs.js`** — the standalone "every proof, every order" list the classic `edit.php?post_type=yp_proof` table gave staff, plus its own Add Proof drawer (a Custom Order `<select>` + file picker, for when you're browsing proofs rather than already inside one order).
- No new CSS needed — Custom Orders and Proofs reuse `.yp-record-table`/`.yp-panel`/`.yp-pill`/`.yp-media-field`/`.yp-drawer--wide` wholesale from `records.css`/`field-schema.css`.

Verify: open the Dashboard and confirm Pending Orders/Pending Proofs/Awaiting Customer Approval/Active Maintenance Subscribers show real data matching the classic reskin's own numbers, with overdue rows flagged; click a Pending Proofs row and confirm it jumps straight into that order's detail; add a proof to a paid order (from both the order detail drawer and the standalone Proofs screen) and confirm the order advances to "Awaiting Proof Approval" and the customer gets the approval email; change an order's status manually and confirm it sticks.

### Fix: opening any Custom Order threw "Couldn't load this order: null is not an object"

Direct report, with the exact console error. `views/orders.js`'s "+ Add Proof" button markup had a stray `"` right before its closing `>` — `data-yp-add-proof">` instead of `data-yp-add-proof>` (compare the correctly-written `data-yp-save-status>` button one line below it in the same panel). HTML's attribute-name tokenizer doesn't treat a bare `"` encountered outside a quoted value as a syntax error that aborts parsing — it silently folds the stray character into the attribute name itself, so the button that actually landed in the DOM carried an attribute literally named `data-yp-add-proof"` (quote included), not `data-yp-add-proof`. `bodyEl.querySelector('[data-yp-add-proof]')`, which matches on the exact attribute name, therefore always returned `null` for every order — paid or not, every single time the detail drawer opened, since that line runs unconditionally inside `renderDetail()`'s `if ( order.paid )` block. The subsequent `.addEventListener()` call on that `null` is exactly the "null is not an object" TypeError reported, caught by `loadDetail()`'s own `.catch()` and displayed as "Couldn't load this order" — a rendering bug one line away from the failure surfaced as if the REST call itself had failed. Fixed by removing the stray quote; grepped the rest of `assets/admin-app/` for the same `data-yp-*">` shape to confirm no other button had the identical typo.

## Custom admin dashboard, Phase 7: Settings, Rewards, Card Surcharge

Direct request: "Onto the next phase." The three remaining standalone Settings-API pages (`class-admin-menu.php`'s general Settings, `class-rewards-admin.php`, `class-surcharge-admin.php`) — each backed by `register_setting()`/`options.php` form posts, none of it REST-reachable at all before this, same "full read/write surface, not a small gap" shape as Phase 6's Custom Orders.

- **`YeffoPrint_Admin_Settings_Controller`** (`GET`/`POST /admin/settings`) — one comprehensive endpoint covering all ~15 options across every section the classic page has (Announcement Bar, Shipment Tracking credentials, Label Configurator live-preview toggle, Homepage Promo, Contact Form, Splash Screen, Dashboard due-date, Maintenance Subscription payment link + Stripe webhook secret) — deliberately not split per-section, since a single Save on the classic page already saves everything together. Same sanitize rules as `class-admin-menu.php`'s own field callbacks (`sanitize_text_field`/`sanitize_email`/`esc_url_raw`/promo-theme-slug validation/etc.), just applied directly against `get_option()`/`update_option()` instead of through the Settings API's `sanitize_callback` pipeline. `promo_themes` (`YeffoPrint_Promo_Themes::all()`, label only — not the full per-theme color palette, which only the banner's own render needs) rides along on every response for the Theme `<select>`.
- **`YeffoPrint_Admin_Surcharge_Controller`** (`GET`/`POST /admin/surcharge`) — the first thing to ever expose `SURCHARGE_GATEWAY_RATES_OPTION` over REST at all (`class-surcharge-admin.php`'s own docblock: deliberately `show_in_rest => false`, "nothing here reads it over REST" — true until now). Same sanitize rule as the classic page's `sanitize_gateway_rates()`: only currently-registered gateway ids, only a strictly-positive rate, everything else dropped rather than saved as a zeroed-out no-op row.
- **`YeffoPrint_Admin_Rewards_Controller`** — four routes covering the classic page's four sections: `GET`/`POST /admin/rewards-settings` (points-per-dollar/dollars-per-point/referral-points), `GET /admin/rewards-history` (the global recent-adjustments log), `GET /admin/rewards-lookup` (one customer's balance + full earn/redeem/adjustment history, same merged shape `YeffoPrint_Rewards::get_history_for_user()` already builds for the My Account Rewards tab), and `POST /admin/rewards-adjust` — a thin wrapper calling the exact same `YeffoPrint_Rewards::adjust_balance()` the classic page's `admin_post_yeffoprint_rewards_adjust` handler already calls, same email-or-username user resolution rule too.
- **`views/settings.js`/`views/surcharge.js`/`views/rewards.js`** — Settings and Card Surcharge are single-record "edit the one active record, no list" screens (`.yp-panel` sections, same shape as `views/pricing.js`); Rewards is four independent sections on one page (a rates-settings panel with its own Save, a lookup box, the adjust form, and the history table), each talking to its own route so e.g. applying an adjustment only ever re-fetches the history log, not the whole page.
- One bug caught during self-review before shipping (same class as Phase 6's stray-quote fix): an early draft of `views/rewards.js`'s customer-lookup history row built the colored points cell by calling the shared `formatDelta()` helper and then string-`.replace()`-ing part of its output to swap in the "+12 / −4" earned/redeemed text — fragile (silently wrong the moment `formatDelta()`'s own markup changed) and confusing to read. Replaced with building that one cell's markup directly instead of routing it through a helper built for a different shape (a single net delta, not an earned/redeemed pair).

Verify: change the announcement bar text and confirm it updates on the storefront immediately; toggle the homepage promo on with a theme/offer/code and confirm the banner appears; set a card gateway's surcharge rate and confirm it shows at checkout; change the rewards points-per-dollar rate and confirm a new order earns the new amount; look up a customer and confirm their balance/history matches what's in Recent Adjustments; apply a manual adjustment and confirm the balance updates everywhere (this screen, the customer's My Account Rewards tab, and the global log).

## Custom admin dashboard, Phase 8: Retirement

Direct request: "Go ahead and finish." Every screen in the plan now has a working, verified replacement in the custom admin app — this phase collapses the classic wp-admin sidebar down to that app as the one entry point, per the plan's own closing step.

**What actually got retired, and what deliberately didn't.** The plan's own wording ("point the old CPT list/edit screens' capability requirement up or simply stop linking to them... delete the CSS reskin and old per-screen JS once nothing renders through them") offered two different levers — capability changes vs. unlinking — and this phase used only the second, for a reason worth recording: every Materials/Sizes/Sticker Sizes/Templates/Field Presets/Web Design Packages/Maintenance Subscribers screen in the new app reads and writes through WP core's own `/wp/v2/{type}` REST routes (Phases 2–5), and those routes authorize using the exact same post-type capabilities (`edit_posts`, `publish_posts`, etc.) the classic wp-admin screens use — there's no separate capability namespace for "the new app's own access" vs. "the classic editor's access" to the same records. Raising the capability requirement high enough to lock administrators out of the classic post.php screens would have locked the new app's own core-REST calls out too, breaking half the phases already shipped. So retirement here means **unlinking, not deleting or capability-gating**:

- **`class-post-type-registry.php`**: `show_in_menu` set to `false` (was the implicit `'yeffoprint'` default) for every post type that used to have its own classic sidebar entry — Templates ("Design Setup"), Pricing Rules, Custom Orders, Proofs, Field Presets, Maintenance Subscribers, Web Design Packages, and `yp_saved_design` (see below). `show_ui` stays `true` on all of them, so `post.php`/`post-new.php`/`edit.php` at their direct URLs — and every classic editor class's meta boxes and `save_post_{type}` save logic, completely untouched — keep working exactly as before. This is a deliberate, disclosed fallback, not a dead end: this environment has no way to click through a live site after a change to confirm nothing broke, so keeping a fully-functional (if unstyled-from-the-sidebar's-perspective) escape hatch was judged the safer trade against deleting a large amount of working code with no way to verify the deletion didn't regress something. Materials/Sizes/Sticker Sizes were already `false` since Phase 1 (folded into the "Design Setup" tab strip) and needed no change.
- **`class-admin-menu.php`**: the explicit "Dashboard" self-link submenu (originally added to work around WordPress's own quirk of auto-inserting a confusing duplicate-labeled entry once *sibling* submenus exist) is removed outright — with every sibling submenu now gone too, that quirk can't trigger anymore, so the top-level "YeffoPrint" sidebar icon is just a plain link straight into the new app, no flyout. The classic Settings page's `add_submenu_page()` call changed its parent from `'yeffoprint'` to `null` — WordPress's own documented idiom for "this page still exists at its direct URL, just isn't shown in any menu," the exact same unlinked-but-functional treatment as the CPT screens above.
- **`class-rewards-admin.php`**/**`class-surcharge-admin.php`**: same `null`-parent treatment for their own `add_submenu_page()` calls. `admin_post_yeffoprint_rewards_adjust` and the Settings API `register_setting()` calls both stay registered and functional — nothing about *reaching* the data changed, only whether a menu click gets you there.
- **`yp_saved_design`**: per the plan's own note, this stays out of scope for the new app — it's customer-owned data with no admin workflow at all (created/read/deleted entirely through REST by the owning customer), so there was never a classic UI role to replace. Hidden from the menu along with everything else for consistency; **flagging for the owner, as the plan asked**, rather than deciding unilaterally: is there ever a need to browse these from wp-admin, or is direct-URL/database access enough?
- **Left untouched entirely**: `YeffoPrint_Admin_Shell`'s reskin (`admin-shell.css`, block-editor/custom-fields-box suppression) and every `assets/admin/*.js` picker script — since the classic editor screens they style/power are being kept as a working fallback rather than deleted, removing their CSS/JS would leave that fallback looking broken rather than simply unlinked. `class-design-setup-menu.php`'s tab strip likewise stays, still functional on its own now-unlinked pages. `YeffoPrint_Dashboard_Widgets` and `class-admin-menu.php`'s own `render_dashboard()`/`quick_links()` were already dead code as of Phase 6 (noted in that class's docblock at the time) and remain so — genuinely superseded, kept as inert code rather than deleted, consistent with everything else this phase chose not to delete.

Verify: confirm the "YeffoPrint" sidebar icon now goes straight to the new Dashboard with no flyout submenu, and that Design Setup/Pricing Rules/Custom Orders/Proofs/Field Presets/Maintenance Subscribers/Web Design Packages/Saved Designs/Settings/Rewards/Card Surcharge are all gone from the sidebar; spot-check that a couple of their direct URLs (e.g. `edit.php?post_type=yp_template`, `admin.php?page=yeffoprint-rewards`) still load and save correctly as an unlinked fallback; full click-through of the new app itself covering every phase's screen, confirming nothing regressed along the way.

## Mark a Material out of stock without removing it from the site

Direct request: "I don't want it to remove it completely from the site, I'd like it to still show, but I'd like an out of stock indicator on it and to stop it from being selected until its back in stock." Deliberately a new field, not a repurposing of `post_status` (already used as "Active/Draft" everywhere in this codebase) — unpublishing a Material hides it entirely, which is a different thing than what was asked for here: still visible, just not selectable.

- **`YeffoPrint_Commerce_Record_Meta::IN_STOCK`** (`_yp_in_stock`) — Material only, boolean, defaults to `true` so every existing Material predates this field without a migration. REST-registered the same way `_yp_material_scope`/etc. already are, so the admin app's Materials screen (`views/materials.js`) needed no new PHP controller — same "core `/wp/v2/yp_material` route already covers it" pattern every Materials/Sizes field has used since Phase 2. New "In stock" checkbox in the add/edit drawer (defaults checked, same idiom as the existing Active checkbox) and an "Out of Stock" pill in the list view alongside the Active/Draft one.
- **All three places a customer picks a Material now carry `in_stock` on every Material they return**: `class-template-schema-controller.php::format_material()` (the main label configurator), `class-custom-order-controller.php`'s Fully Custom Design batch-row materials (split out of a `$format` closure it used to share with Sizes, since Sizes have no stock concept), and `class-custom-sticker-controller.php`'s `$material_format`. An out-of-stock Material is never filtered out of these lists — it has to keep showing, per the request — only flagged.
- **Front end**: the main configurator's Material pills (`configurator.js`) render a real `disabled` button (blocks click and keyboard activation for free, no extra guard code needed in the click handler) with "Out of Stock" in place of the price-adjustment text and a dimmed/grayscale treatment (`configurator.css`'s new `.yp-option-pill--out-of-stock`); the page's own default-Material selection (`firstAvailableMaterialId()`) now skips out-of-stock entries so a fresh page load never lands on an unselectable default, falling back to the first Material regardless of stock only if literally everything is out. The Fully Custom Design and Custom Stickers forms use native `<select>` elements instead of pills, so the same idea there is a `disabled` `<option>` with "(Out of Stock)" appended to its label — browsers already skip a disabled option when picking a default, so those two forms needed no separate selected-value-skipping logic (`custom-order-form.js`'s batch-row default did still need the same `firstAvailableMaterialId()` treatment, since it tracks `row.material_id` as its own JS state rather than always reading the live `<select>.value`).
- **Server-side enforcement everywhere a Material can actually be chosen** — `class-cart-controller.php` (label add-to-cart), `class-custom-order-controller.php` (Fully Custom Design submission), and `class-custom-sticker-controller.php` (Custom Stickers submission) each already validated a submitted `material_id` exists and is published; all three now also reject one that's out of stock, same defense-in-depth reasoning as every other server-side check in this codebase — the disabled pill/option is a UX nicety, not the actual gate.

Verify: mark a Material out of stock, confirm it still appears everywhere it did before (configurator, Custom Stickers, Fully Custom Design) with a clear "Out of Stock" indicator and can't be selected through the UI; confirm a direct API request choosing that Material is rejected; mark it back in stock and confirm it's selectable again immediately.

## Package tracking, part 1: a real "Shipped" status + a Dashboard panel for it

Direct request: "I'd like to see all of my packages that have not been delivered yet with current tracking status... I'd like order status to change from processing to shipped when I create a shipping label using WooCommerce Shipping." Scoped into two parts up front, in the terminal rather than as code: the Processing→Shipped half needs nothing this store doesn't already have; the later Shipped→Delivered half needs a working USPS or UPS tracking API connection, which isn't set up yet (the existing `YeffoPrint_Usps_Tracking_Provider`/`YeffoPrint_Ups_Tracking_Provider` classes were built ahead of credentials and have their own docblocks flagging their event-field assumptions as unverified against a real response) — so this entry is the first half only.

- **`YeffoPrint_Order_Shipment_Status`** (`includes/woocommerce/class-order-shipment-status.php`) registers a real custom WooCommerce order status, `wc-shipped` ("Shipped"), inserted right after "Processing" in every status dropdown/list via the standard `register_post_status()` + `wc_order_statuses` filter recipe — a real status, not a label bolted onto something else, so staff can also set/clear it manually from the normal WooCommerce order screen if the automation ever needs a manual nudge.
- **Detection deliberately doesn't hook any specific WooCommerce Shipping action.** Same reasoning `class-order-tracking.php`'s own docblock already gives for reading that plugin's label meta directly instead of adding a second, competing place to enter a tracking number: rather than guess at (and risk getting wrong, or having drift under a WC Shipping update) whatever internal action fires when a label is purchased, `maybe_advance_to_shipped()` hooks WooCommerce's own stable `woocommerce_after_order_object_save` — fired on every order save, by any code path — and simply checks whether `YeffoPrint_Order_Tracking::get_shipments( $order )` (the exact same normalized shipment list the customer-facing tracking page already reads) has changed since the last time this ran, via a hash stored in a new `_yp_shipment_fingerprint` meta key. An order still "Processing" whose shipment list just changed moves to "Shipped"; anything else is a no-op, so the hook stays cheap on the overwhelming majority of the order saves it fires on for reasons that have nothing to do with shipping. `set_status()` + `save()` re-enters this exact method through the same hook it's already running inside of — safe, since by then `$order` (the same in-memory object) already reflects "shipped" and the very first guard exits immediately.
- **`class-admin-dashboard-controller.php`** gains a fifth Dashboard section, `shipped_packages` — every order currently "Shipped", one row per shipment rather than per order (a single order can carry more than one label, and "all of my packages" means every physical package). Deliberately carries no overdue-days flagging the way the other four sections do: that needs a real "label purchased" timestamp this store doesn't reliably have (WooCommerce Shipping's own label data only guarantees a tracking number), and using the order date instead would just be a misleading proxy for how long a package has actually been in transit. Each row still links out to the tracking number's direct carrier URL (`YeffoPrint_Order_Tracking::carrier_direct_url()`, already built, needs no API credentials) — a live in-page status per row is exactly the piece waiting on part 2.

Verify: buy a real (or test) shipping label through WooCommerce Shipping on a "Processing" order, confirm it moves to "Shipped" automatically and shows up in the new Dashboard panel with a working tracking link; confirm "Shipped" appears in the order's own status dropdown for a manual override if ever needed.

## Telegram bot: FAQs + order-status lookup

Direct request: "create a telegram bot that connects to my Wordpress/woocommerce site to answer basic questions and track customer order status." Built as a new `includes/telegram/` module in `yeffoprint-core` — a REST webhook, not a standalone long-running process — the same shape as the existing Venmo/Zelle and Stripe webhook integrations (`class-payment-webhook-controller.php`, `class-stripe-webhook-controller.php`), which fits this store's actual hosting (ordinary WordPress request/response, nothing that needs to stay running) far better than a polling bot process would.

- **`YeffoPrint_Telegram_Webhook_Controller`** (`includes/rest/`) registers `POST yeffoprint-core/v1/telegram/webhook`. Authenticated by the `X-Telegram-Bot-Api-Secret-Token` header Telegram echoes back on every call — Telegram's own documented webhook-security mechanism — checked with `hash_equals()` against a generate-once secret (`class-telegram-webhook-secret.php`, same pattern as the payment webhook's bearer token). Always replies `200 { ok: true }` regardless of outcome, including a rate-limited or unrecognized update — a non-2xx response makes Telegram retry the same update indefinitely, which is never the right behavior for "nothing to do" cases. Per-chat rate limiting (20 messages / 5 minutes, a transient) exists specifically to blunt someone hammering `/order` to brute-force order numbers, since the bot itself has no login wall.
- **`YeffoPrint_Telegram_Message_Handler`** is pure command-routing/reply-text logic, deliberately separated from the webhook controller's Telegram-plumbing (JSON update shape, secret-token check, actually calling `sendMessage`) so the actual bot behavior has no HTTP/Telegram-API concerns mixed in. Recognizes `/start`, `/help`, `/order` (aliases `/track`/`/status`), `/faq`, and free text — a plain message containing both an email and a number (with or without a leading command) is treated as an order lookup, since that's the natural way to answer the bot's own prompt for one.
- **`YeffoPrint_Telegram_Order_Lookup`** — the same guest-access rule WooCommerce's own native order-tracking shortcode uses: order number + the exact billing email from checkout, nothing else. A Telegram chat has no session/nonce/order_key the way `/track-order/` (`class-order-tracking-controller.php`) does, so the email match *is* the entire access control; a wrong guess returns the same "couldn't find that" reply whether the order exists or not, so no order's existence (or lack of it) leaks to a stranger. Status replies reuse `YeffoPrint_Order_Tracking::get_shipments()` verbatim for carrier/tracking info (never a second, competing place that reads shipment data) and additionally surface the linked Custom Order's own production status (`YeffoPrint_Custom_Order_Meta::STATUS`) when the WooCommerce order came from that flow — found via a `meta_query` on `_yp_wc_order_id`, since no reverse lookup from WC order → Custom Order post existed yet.
- **`YeffoPrint_Telegram_Faq`** — plain keyword-overlap matching (count keyword hits per topic, highest score wins) against the same five FAQ topics `yeffoprint/patterns/faq.php` already shows on the homepage. Copy is duplicated, not read from the theme — PROJECT_SPEC §3 requires business-critical functionality (this plugin) to never depend on the active theme, and reading a theme pattern file from the plugin would invert that.
- **Settings**: two new options, `TELEGRAM_BOT_TOKEN_OPTION`/`TELEGRAM_ENABLED_OPTION`, added to `class-admin-menu.php` alongside every other Settings-screen option — a "Telegram Bot" panel in the admin-app's `views/settings.js` (paste a bot token from @BotFather, check "Bot is active") plus the matching classic Settings-API section as an unlinked fallback, same dual-surface pattern as everything else on that screen since Phase 8's retirement. A token can also be set via a `YEFFOPRINT_TELEGRAM_BOT_TOKEN` wp-config.php constant instead, for an admin who'd rather keep it out of the database — checked first by `YeffoPrint_Telegram_Settings::get_bot_token()`.
- **`YeffoPrint_Telegram_Webhook_Sync`** hooks `add_option_*`/`update_option_*` on both Telegram options rather than living inside `class-admin-settings-controller.php`'s save handler — both the classic Settings page (`options.php`) and the admin-app's REST save end up calling `update_option()` on the same two keys, so hooking once there covers both save paths automatically instead of duplicating "now call Telegram" logic in two places. Turning the bot on calls `setWebhook`; turning it off (or clearing the token) calls `deleteWebhook` for real, not just a local ignore-updates flag — unchecking "active" needed to be a genuine off switch. The result message ("Connected — the bot is live." / a Telegram error / "Bot is off") is stored in its own option and shown back on both the admin-app panel and the classic page's section intro.
- **`wp yeffoprint telegram sync-webhook` / `wp yeffoprint telegram info`** — manual fallbacks for the case the options-hook path can't cover (a token set only via the wp-config.php constant never fires `update_option`) and a way to check Telegram's own `getWebhookInfo` view without leaving the terminal, same "dev-only setup command" idiom as `setup-shipping`/`setup-pages`.

Verify: create a bot via @BotFather, paste its token into Settings → Telegram Bot, check "Bot is active", save, and confirm the panel shows "Connected — the bot is live."; message the bot `/start` and confirm the help text arrives; ask a FAQ-shaped question ("what materials do you have?") and confirm a matching topic answer comes back; send a real order's number + its checkout email (e.g. `YP-1042 jane@example.com`) and confirm the status/tracking reply matches that order, and that a wrong email for a real order number returns the same "couldn't find it" reply as a nonexistent order number; uncheck "Bot is active" and confirm Telegram's `getWebhookInfo` (`wp yeffoprint telegram info`) shows no URL registered.

### Follow-up: the bot can answer bulk pricing/discount questions

Direct request: "can the bot answer questions about bulk discounts? It should be able to answer questions about the pricing tiers." A new sixth FAQ topic (`pricing_answer()`), but deliberately not written as static copy the way the other five are — pricing tiers are admin-edited (Pricing Rule editor, `includes/pricing/class-pricing-rule.php`) and change over time, so hardcoded numbers here would silently drift out of sync the next time someone edited a tier. Instead it reads `YeffoPrint_Pricing_Rule::get_base_unit_price()`/`get_tiers()` live, on every match — the exact same authoritative source the storefront configurator and checkout price off of — and mirrors that class's own `calculate()` logic for what a tier actually resolves to (a `percent` tier discounts the base rate; `fixed_unit_price` sets it directly), rather than re-deriving the math a second way that could disagree with it.

The reply lists each configured tier's resulting per-label price ("500+ labels: $0.28 each"), plus a line restating the mix-and-match rule the existing batch-FAQ entry already states ("combined quantity still counts toward bulk pricing") — since a customer asking about bulk pricing is exactly the person who needs to know that splitting one order across several designs still qualifies. Falls back to a plain base-price sentence if no tiers are configured yet, rather than showing an empty list. `topics_text()` (the `/help`/`/faq` reply) gained a matching "Bulk pricing & discounts" line so it's actually discoverable, not just reachable by accident.

Verify: set at least two bulk discount tiers in the Pricing Rule editor (e.g. 100+ at 10% off, 500+ at 20% off) → message the bot "do you offer bulk discounts?" or "what are your pricing tiers?" → confirm the reply lists each tier's resulting per-label price matching what the storefront configurator itself shows at those quantities → change a tier's value in the editor → ask again → confirm the bot's answer reflects the new number immediately, with no cache to bust → clear all tiers → ask again → confirm it falls back to the plain base-price sentence instead of an empty list → send `/help` → confirm "Bulk pricing & discounts" appears in the topic list.

## "In Production" status + a Dashboard "Send to Printer" button

Direct request: "Can we add another order status, 'In Production' and maybe a button on our admin dashboard that says 'Send to Printer' next to any pending orders that are in processing status? Pressing that just changes its order status to in production, doesn't actually print anything." Purely a manual status marker — no printer/hardware integration of any kind, by explicit instruction.

- **`YeffoPrint_Order_Production_Status`** (`includes/woocommerce/class-order-production-status.php`) registers `wc-in-production` ("In Production") the same `register_post_status()` + `wc_order_statuses` filter recipe as `class-order-shipment-status.php`, slotted right after "Processing" — the real pipeline is now Processing → In Production → Shipped → Completed. Unlike Shipped, nothing here transitions an order automatically; the only thing that ever sets this status is the new REST action below (staff can still also set/clear it by hand from the normal WooCommerce order screen, same as any status).
- **`class-order-shipment-status.php`'s own `add_to_status_list()` updated**: it used to anchor "Shipped" right after "Processing"; now it anchors after "In Production" when that status is present (falling back to "Processing" if it's somehow missing), so the two new/existing statuses don't fight over the same insertion point and the dropdown always reads in real pipeline order regardless of which of the two filters WordPress happens to run first.
- **`YeffoPrint_Admin_Order_Controller`** (`POST /admin/order/{id}/send-to-printer`) — the first (and so far only) direct-action endpoint on a native WooCommerce order from this app's REST layer; everything else about WC orders still stays in classic wp-admin per `class-admin-dashboard-controller.php`'s own docblock. Validates the order is still "Processing" (409 if not — guards against a double-click or two staff acting on the same order) and moves it to "In Production" via `set_status()` + `save()`, the same primitive `class-order-shipment-status.php` already uses.
- **`class-admin-dashboard-controller.php`'s `pending_wc_orders()`** now includes each order's `id`, so the Dashboard can address a specific order without adding a second query.
- **Dashboard home (`app.js`)**: `dashboardSectionHtml()` gained an optional `rowAction` callback parameter — only the Pending Orders panel passes one (a "Send to Printer" button per row); Pending Proofs/Awaiting Customer Approval are unaffected. The button POSTs to the new endpoint and reloads the whole dashboard summary on success, so the row simply disappears from Pending Orders on its own (that panel only ever queries "Processing" orders) rather than needing any client-side row removal logic.

Verify: on a "Processing" order, click "Send to Printer" on the Dashboard and confirm the order moves to "In Production" (both in the order's own status dropdown and by the row dropping off the Pending Orders panel); confirm "In Production" sits between "Processing" and "Shipped" in the status dropdown/order list; confirm buying a shipping label on an order already in "In Production" still advances it to "Shipped" same as before.

## Telegram bot, part 2: admin alerts, escalation, reorder links, proactive status pings

Direct follow-up: "What can it do? I want it to handle as much as it can!" — offered four concrete additions and the owner picked all four. Each reuses an existing mechanism rather than inventing a parallel one, in keeping with how the rest of this integration was built.

- **Admin alerts** (`YeffoPrint_Telegram_Admin_Alerts`) — a new paid order (or custom design request) and a Contact form submission now also push a live Telegram message to the owner's own chat, as a faster companion to the existing email notifications, not a replacement. Hooks the exact events those email paths already fire on: `woocommerce_payment_complete` (one hook covers both plain and custom-design orders — checks each line item for `_yp_custom_order_id` to label the alert correctly, no second event needed) and a new `yeffoprint_contact_form_submitted` action added to the end of `class-contact-controller.php::send()`. A new `TELEGRAM_ADMIN_CHAT_ID_OPTION` holds the destination; a new `/whoami` (alias `/id`) bot command exists purely so the owner can message the bot once to learn their own chat's numeric id, no separate BotFather step needed.
- **Escalate to a human** (`YeffoPrint_Telegram_Escalation`) — when a message matches neither an order lookup nor an FAQ topic, the bot now offers to relay it instead of just repeating "email us." Deliberately two-step, not automatic-on-every-miss: the offer stores the unmatched text in a short transient (`class-contact-controller.php`'s own rate-limit tool, reused here for the same reason — per-chat state that doesn't need a database row) and only forwards it — by email to the same Contact-form recipient, plus an admin Telegram alert — once the customer replies with something affirmative. A stray "lol" or test message this way never reaches the owner's inbox.
- **Reorder links** (`YeffoPrint_Reorder::urls_for_order()`) — a new `/reorder <order number> <email>` command, using the same guest-access rule as `/order`. The per-item reorder-link logic `render_reorder_link()` already prints inline on the order-detail page was extracted into a static, order-wide method (`should_render_link_for_item()` made `static` too, so nothing needed re-deriving) rather than duplicated — the bot calls the exact same batching-aware dedup logic that page already relies on, just collected into one array instead of echoed per item.
- **Proactive status pings** (`YeffoPrint_Telegram_Order_Notifications`) — no separate opt-in command: the first time a customer successfully checks an order's status through the bot, that chat is linked to the order (`_yp_telegram_chat_ids`, a WC order meta array) for free, and the status reply says so. Linking piggybacks on the exact same order-number + checkout-email check `/order` already requires, so it introduces no new access rule. Two hooks fire the actual pings: `woocommerce_order_status_shipped` (the real custom order status Part 1 of Package Tracking already added) reuses `YeffoPrint_Order_Tracking::get_shipments()` verbatim, and a new `yeffoprint_proof_ready_for_review` action — added to `class-proof-meta.php::advance_status_to_awaiting_approval()`, fired independently of whether that method's own customer email actually sends — reuses the exact same guest-access proof-approval URL (`yeffoprint_core_proof_approval_url()`) that email already links to.

Verify: message the bot `/whoami`, paste the number into Settings → Telegram Bot → "Your chat ID," save, then place and pay for a test order — confirm a Telegram alert arrives; submit the Contact form and confirm a second alert arrives; send the bot something it can't answer, confirm it offers to forward, reply "yes," and confirm both an email and a Telegram alert land; send `/reorder <order> <email>` for a real order and confirm the link(s) match what that order's own detail page shows; check an order's status via the bot, then move it to Shipped (or advance a linked Custom Order's proof) and confirm the same chat gets a follow-up message with no further action needed.

## "Continue with Google" / "Continue with Discord" on the login form

Direct request: "allow users to login with Google/Apple/Discord." Scoped into two parts up front, in the terminal rather than as code — Google and Discord are each a free, few-minutes developer-app registration; Apple additionally needs a paid Apple Developer Program membership ($99/year), domain verification, and a private key that has to be re-signed into a new client secret every 6 months. The owner picked "Google + Discord now, Apple later," so this entry is Google and Discord only — Apple is a deliberate, explicitly deferred follow-up, not forgotten.

- **`YeffoPrint_Social_Login`** (`includes/accounts/class-social-login.php`) is a hand-built OAuth 2.0 Authorization Code flow, not a third-party social-login plugin — same "minimal third-party dependencies" reasoning (PROJECT_SPEC §1.6) as every other external integration in this plugin (Stripe, Telegram, the carrier tracking APIs, the Venmo/Zelle gateways). Browser-navigated via plain `template_redirect` query-var handlers (`?yp-oauth=google` starts the flow, `?yp-oauth-callback=google` is the fixed redirect URI registered with the provider), not REST — same reasoning `class-account-endpoints.php`'s own docblock already gives for its POST-and-redirect handlers: this is the browser being redirected twice over, not a fetch()/JSON exchange.
- **CSRF + carrying `redirect_to` across the round trip** both ride on one random one-time token: starting the flow stashes `redirect_to` in a transient keyed by that token and sends the token as the OAuth `state` param; the callback can only proceed if it finds (and immediately deletes) that exact transient, so a forged or replayed callback is rejected before any code exchange happens.
- **Account matching**: a returning identity (provider + external id already seen) logs straight in. A brand-new identity whose email matches an existing native account auto-links to it — safe because both providers only ever hand back an email they've themselves verified (`normalize_userinfo()` checks Google's `email_verified` / Discord's `verified` explicitly and refuses an unverified one), the same trust basis every mainstream social-login implementation relies on. Anything else creates a new customer through WooCommerce's own `wc_create_new_customer()` — not a hand-rolled `wp_insert_user()` — so a social signup goes through the exact same account-creation path, hooks, and role assignment as a normal one, including `class-referrals.php`'s `user_register` attribution (confirmed by inspection: referral attribution is purely cookie-based, entirely independent of how the account got created). A random 32-character password is generated and never surfaced anywhere — the customer simply never needs one.
- **Settings** (`class-admin-menu.php`, a new "Social Login" section): per provider, an "on" checkbox plus Client ID/Secret — mirrors the existing UPS/USPS/Telegram credential fields exactly, reusing the same `render_secret_field()` helper. A provider's button is only ever rendered once both its checkbox is on *and* a real Client ID is present, so there's no way to end up with a dead button pointing at an unconfigured provider. The section intro shows each provider's exact redirect URI to paste into its developer console.
- **The buttons themselves** render via `woocommerce_login_form_end` — the one hook inside WooCommerce's shared login form template (`templates/myaccount/form-login.php`), so this automatically covers both `/my-account/`'s login form and checkout's "Returning customer? Click here to login" toggle with no extra work. On checkout specifically, the button carries `redirect_to=` the checkout URL so a guest who logs in mid-checkout lands back there instead of My Account. Hand-authored inline SVG icons, no external icon font/CDN — same reasoning as every other inline SVG already in this plugin.

Verify: register a Google OAuth app (redirect URI shown on the Settings page), paste the Client ID/Secret, turn it on, and confirm "Continue with Google" appears on `/my-account/`; click it, approve on Google's consent screen, and confirm you land back logged in as a new customer with that Google account linked; log out and click it again, confirming it logs the same account straight back in; do the same for Discord; start checkout as a guest, use the login toggle's Google/Discord button, and confirm you land back on checkout still logged in rather than My Account.

### Fix: the button didn't appear on the live site at all

Direct report, with each obvious cause ruled out by the owner directly: the deploy had landed (confirmed — other features from the same deploy worked), the settings had actually saved (confirmed by reloading the Settings screen), and there was no page cache in front of the site. View-source showed a completely normal-looking login form with nothing unusual about it — just missing the button.

That combination points at one specific remaining explanation: this install's `myaccount/form-login.php` is a template that predates this project's move to the git-based deploy (`docs/deploy-setup.md`) — `deploy/pull-and-deploy.sh` only ever `git reset --hard`s the tracked files, it never `git clean`s, so any file that was already sitting in that directory before the git clone took over just stays there, forever invisible to this repository, silently shadowing whatever WooCommerce's own template would have rendered. That old template renders a normal-looking form, it just never calls `woocommerce_login_form_end` — the one hook `render_login_buttons()` depended on.

Rather than chase down and hand-edit that one server's specific template file (this environment has no access to it to even confirm what's in it), `enqueue_fallback_script()` makes the button's appearance independent of that hook firing at all. `render_login_buttons()` was refactored to build its markup through a new `buttons_html()` method instead of echoing inline; `enqueue_fallback_script()` (hooked to `wp_enqueue_scripts`, gated to account/checkout pages) calls that same method and, whenever it returns something, hands the exact HTML string to a new `assets/frontend/social-login-inject.js` via `wp_localize_script()`. That script finds the login form by WooCommerce's own standard `.woocommerce-form-login` class — present on every WC login form regardless of which template rendered it — and appends the markup directly, guarded against double-appending wherever the server-side hook *does* fire normally (a fresh/stock install, for instance). Belt and suspenders: the natural hook stays in place for stores that don't have this problem, and the fallback quietly covers the ones that do.

Verify: reload `/my-account/` logged out on the affected site and confirm the buttons now appear; view-source and confirm `yp-social-login` now shows up (via the injected `<script>` + `assets/frontend/social-login-inject.js`, not `woocommerce_login_form_end` directly, on this particular install); confirm clicking through still completes a real login exactly as before.

### Follow-up: the buttons also need to be on `wp-login.php`

Direct report, once the above landed: "Our code redirects all logins to wp-login.php" — this store's actual login flow funnels through WordPress's own native login screen, not the WooCommerce account page the original build targeted. A genuinely different screen, not a variant of the same bug: `wp-login.php` is built into WP core itself, not a theme-overridable template, so there was never a "some other file is shadowing this" question here the way there was for the WooCommerce template.

- **`render_wp_login_buttons()`** hooks WordPress core's own `login_form` action — the hook fired inside `wp-login.php`'s `<form id="loginform">`, right before its submit button, used by essentially every login/SSO/2FA plugin for exactly this purpose. Unlike a theme template, this can't be silently shadowed the way `myaccount/form-login.php` was.
- **`buttons_html()`** gained an optional explicit `$redirect_to` parameter — `render_wp_login_buttons()` passes wp-login.php's own `redirect_to` query arg (the same one a normal username/password login on that page already respects) rather than falling through to the WooCommerce-checkout guess, which doesn't apply on this screen at all.
- **`enqueue_wp_login_styles()`** loads a new, small, self-contained stylesheet (`assets/frontend/social-login-wp-login.css`) via WordPress's own `login_enqueue_scripts` hook — `wp-login.php` never loads the active theme's assets, so the storefront's `woocommerce.css` design tokens aren't available there; colors are picked to sit naturally in WordPress's own default login screen instead of forcing the storefront's branding onto what's really an administrative surface.

Verify: log out and visit the store's normal login link (`wp-login.php?redirect_to=...`) — confirm both buttons appear, styled reasonably against WordPress's default login screen; click through and confirm you land back at the `redirect_to` URL, logged in, exactly like a normal password login on that same page would.

### Follow-up: a "Connected Accounts" indicator on the user profile screen

Direct report + question: the owner logged in with Google and landed in their own admin account, and asked whether that was because the Google account's email matched their admin email (it was — confirmed, and called out explicitly in this class's own docblock now, since email-match auto-linking applies to every WordPress user, administrators included, not just customers) and whether there's any indicator of which accounts have a social login connected.

**`render_profile_section()`** answers that directly: a "Connected Accounts" section on the native WordPress Profile/Edit User screen (`show_user_profile`/`edit_user_profile` — WordPress fires whichever one applies, your own profile vs. an admin viewing someone else's), reading the same `_yp_social_{provider}_id` user meta `find_or_create_user()` already writes. Read-only, listing which provider(s) are linked (or a plain "logs in with a password only" when none are) — same "surface real status, don't hide it" reasoning as every other indicator already in this codebase (e.g. Materials' In Stock/Out of Stock pill).

Verify: open your own Profile screen (and, as an admin, another user's Edit User screen) and confirm "Connected Accounts" shows "Signed in with: Google" for an account that's actually linked, and the no-connection message for one that isn't.

## "Continue with Apple" — the deferred third provider

Direct request: "let's go ahead and get Sign in with Apple built" — the follow-up to the owner's own earlier "Google + Discord now, Apple later" choice, now that they're ready for Apple's bigger setup lift (paid Apple Developer Program membership, domain verification). Apple's own OAuth/OIDC flow is structurally different from Google's and Discord's in two ways this class had to account for everywhere, not just in `provider_config()` — both are explained in full in the class's own top docblock, summarized here:

- **No static client secret.** Apple wants a JWT, signed with an ES256 (P-256 elliptic-curve) private key downloaded once from Apple Developer, as the "client_secret" on every token exchange. The conventional approach is to generate that JWT once and reuse it until Apple's 6-month cap forces a rotation — exactly the operational burden flagged as the reason Apple was deferred in the first place. `generate_apple_client_secret()` sidesteps it entirely: it mints a fresh JWT, valid for a few minutes, immediately before every single token exchange, so there's never a long-lived secret sitting anywhere to expire or need rotating. Signing it uses PHP's OpenSSL extension directly (`openssl_sign()` with `OPENSSL_ALGO_SHA256`) — no JWT library, consistent with this plugin's "minimal third-party dependencies" stance and the fact that there's no Composer/vendor setup anywhere in this project to hang one off of. The one genuinely fiddly piece is `der_ecdsa_signature_to_raw()`: OpenSSL hands back an ASN.1 DER-encoded signature, but the JWS ES256 spec wants the raw 64-byte r‖s concatenation instead, so it's unwrapped by hand — the same well-known conversion essentially every hand-rolled ES256 JWT implementation (in any language) has to do.
- **The callback can arrive as a POST.** Apple requires `response_mode=form_post` whenever the scope includes `name`/`email` (both needed here) — Apple's server returns an auto-submitting HTML form to the browser that POSTs `code`/`state`/(first authorization only) `user` to the redirect URI, rather than a query-string GET redirect the way Google/Discord work. `request_param()` (checks `$_POST` first, `$_GET` second) let `callback()` handle both transports with one implementation rather than forking the whole method — Google/Discord's GET-based callback needed no changes at all. Apple also has no separate userinfo endpoint; identity comes from decoding the `id_token` JWT the token endpoint returns alongside the access token (`decode_id_token()` — payload only, no signature verification, trusted on the same basis this class already trusts a Google/Discord userinfo response: it arrived over a direct server-to-server HTTPS call this code itself just made to the provider, never having passed through the browser). The one-time `user` POST field (Apple's only chance to hand over a name — every later login carries just the stable `sub`) is captured opportunistically in `callback()` when present.

**Settings** (`class-admin-menu.php`, same "Social Login" section as Google/Discord): Apple needs meaningfully more than the other two — a Services ID (its "Client ID"), Team ID, Key ID, and the full .p8 private key contents (a multi-line PEM value, so its own `<textarea>` field rather than the shared single-line `render_secret_field()`, and `sanitize_textarea_field()` rather than `sanitize_text_field()` so the key's line breaks survive saving). The section intro now shows Apple's return URL alongside Google/Discord's redirect URIs, plus a note that it needs a paid Developer Program membership and domain verification.

Verify: register an Apple Services ID with Sign in with Apple enabled, verify the domain (hosting the association file Apple's console provides, at the exact path it specifies — not something this deploy manages, since it lives outside `wp-content` entirely, same category as `robots.txt`), create a signing key and note its Key ID, set the return URL to the one shown on the Settings page, paste in the Services ID/Team ID/Key ID/private key, turn Apple on, and confirm "Continue with Apple" appears everywhere the other two do (`/my-account/`, checkout's login toggle, `wp-login.php`); click through and confirm a real login/signup completes, including on a second login (proving the JWT client secret is being minted correctly on every request, not just the first).

### Fix: no breathing room below the buttons

Direct report, with a screenshot of `wp-login.php`: "Continue with Google" sat pressed right up against the Remember Me checkbox below it, no gap at all. `.yp-social-login` (both `assets/frontend/social-login-wp-login.css` and the theme's `woocommerce.css`) only ever had a `margin-top`, never a `margin-bottom` — the block had breathing room from whatever came before it, but nothing pushing the next element away. Added a matching bottom margin in both stylesheets.

### Fix, part 2: the margin-bottom patch didn't actually fix it — the real story

Direct report, with a follow-up screenshot after that patch deployed: still no real gap, just relocated — now Remember Me and the Log In button were pressed together instead. That was the tell that the margin-bottom fix had treated a symptom, not the cause.

Two things came out of actually digging in:

1. **This store already has a fully custom-branded `wp-login.php`** — `yeffoprint/assets/css/login.css`, enqueued via `functions.php`'s own `login_enqueue_scripts` hook, predating this feature and built in an earlier, since-summarized part of this session. It also already **redirects every other login entry point to `wp-login.php`** (`functions.php`'s `template_redirect` hook), which is exactly what "our code redirects all logins to wp-login.php" — the direct report that got the wp-login.php buttons built in the first place — was describing. `class-social-login.php`'s own docblock had an incorrect assumption ("wp-login.php never loads the active theme's assets") baked in from before this was known, which is why the wp-login.php buttons were styled with a separate, generic, plugin-owned stylesheet instead of this theme's real brand tokens — visibly mismatched against the branded card/gradient-button/dark-backdrop treatment everywhere else on that page.
2. **The actual layout bug**: WordPress core floats `.forgetmenot` (Remember Me) left, and `login.css`'s `.submit { clear: both; }` relies on that float to position the Log In button below it. Floated elements are skipped entirely when the browser calculates margin collapsing between normal-flow siblings — so once a new block (`.yp-social-login`) was inserted immediately before the floated `.forgetmenot`, the browser started collapsing *that new block's* margin with `.submit`'s margin instead of accounting for the float's real height, and the clearance that used to create a healthy gap shrank to almost nothing. This is exactly the kind of interaction that only shows up once something changes what's adjacent to a floated element — a pre-existing fragility in the float/clear approach, not a bug in the original page.

The real fix: removed the float entirely (`.forgetmenot { float: none; }`, and the now-meaningless `clear: both` on `.submit`) — the submit button was already `width: 100%` and everything already rendered full-width/stacked, so the float was doing no layout work to begin with, just quietly setting up this exact fragility. Normal block stacking with ordinary margins is immune to whatever gets inserted before it in the future. `.yp-social-login`'s styling moved into `login.css` itself, reskinned with the page's actual tokens (Inter font, cyan `#00AEEF` accent border/focus, near-black/charcoal text, light-gray borders, `radius--input`) instead of the generic gray-bordered version — same division of labor already used for the storefront/checkout version of these buttons (styled in `woocommerce.css`, not the plugin): the plugin (`class-social-login.php`) only ever outputs the `.yp-social-login` markup, styling any front-end surface is the theme's job. The now-redundant `assets/frontend/social-login-wp-login.css` and its `enqueue_wp_login_styles()` enqueue were deleted rather than left as dead code.

Verify: reload `wp-login.php` logged out and confirm a real, even gap both above and below the social login buttons, and that the button styling now matches the rest of the branded card (Inter font, cyan focus/hover, not generic system-gray); resize/check on mobile specifically, since that's where the collapsed gap was most visible.

### Fix, part 3: moved the social buttons below Log In, and made the spacing fix permanent

Direct follow-up, with a third screenshot after part 2 deployed: the content had shifted down, but Remember Me-to-Log In was still tight, and the actual ask was different from another positional patch — move "or continue with" and the social buttons to appear *below* the Log In button entirely, not just fix the gap around wherever they currently sit.

The obstacle: WordPress core fires the `login_form` action (what `render_wp_login_buttons()` hooks) right after the password field — well before Remember Me and Log In — and there's no hook that fires later while still inside `<form id="loginform">`. The buttons' DOM position is fixed by WordPress core, not something this plugin controls.

Fix, entirely in `login.css`: `body.login #loginform` is now `display: flex; flex-direction: column;`, and `.yp-social-login` gets `order: 10` — every other child keeps the default `order: 0` (so Username/Password/Remember Me/Log In keep their natural DOM order), and the one explicitly reordered child jumps to the visual end regardless of where `login_form` actually fired. This also closes the door on the part 2 bug's whole category, for free: flex item margins never collapse with each other (per spec), so nothing inserted before `.forgetmenot` in the DOM can ever again eat into `.submit`'s spacing the way it did before — the now-pointless `float: none` override on `.forgetmenot` was simplified away since a flex layout already neutralizes float/clear on its children without needing to say so explicitly.

Verify: reload `wp-login.php` logged out and confirm the visual order is Username → Password → Remember Me → Log In → "or continue with" → social buttons, with an even, non-cramped gap around each; resize/check on mobile.

### Fix, part 4: the actual reason the gap kept looking thin across all three earlier attempts

Direct follow-up, with a fourth screenshot: buttons correctly below Log In (part 3 worked), but Remember Me→Log In was *still* visually tight.

The real cause had been sitting in plain sight since part 1: this file's own header comment says some rules need `!important` to beat `wp-admin/css/login.min.css`'s own ID-anchored selectors — and nearly every rule that touches a core-styled element in this file already carries it (`#loginform`, the inputs, `#wp-submit`, the notice boxes). `.forgetmenot`'s `margin-bottom` and `.submit`'s `margin-top` — the two rules directly responsible for this exact gap, in every one of the last three fix attempts — never got it. Core's own spacing on those elements, set via a more specific ID-anchored selector, was winning outright regardless of what value this theme's rule set it to; flex non-collapsing (part 3) fixed a real bug but was never going to fix this one, since the losing rule wasn't being applied at all.

Fixed by adding `!important` to both (and bumping both to `1.5rem` to match the vertical rhythm already used elsewhere on this card, e.g. `.yp-social-login`'s own margin-top).

Verify: reload `wp-login.php` logged out and confirm a clearly visible, even gap between Remember Me and Log In — not just present, but obviously not cramped against the neighboring elements.

## Site migration: total database + media backup/restore (direct request: "update our migration plugin to do a total backup of the entire site and restore of the entire site, media and all," ahead of moving to a new server)

No migration/backup plugin actually existed anywhere in the codebase (checked every plugin directory and all of `yeffoprint-core` itself) — "our migration plugin" turned out to mean this project's own plugin, `yeffoprint-core`, which this adds a new capability to.

Scoped down from "the entire site" to database + `wp-content/uploads` specifically: the plugin/theme code is already handled by this project's own git-based deploy (`docs/deploy-setup.md`), which the owner is re-syncing on the new server themselves — duplicating code into a backup archive would just be dead weight riding along with the actual data that needs moving.

Confirmed with the owner before building: shell/WP-CLI access is available on both the old and new server, and the media library is a few GB. That ruled out a browser-based "download backup" admin screen — a multi-gigabyte archive pushed through an HTTP response/upload is exactly the kind of thing that hits PHP memory limits, `max_execution_time`, and upload-size caps, none of which apply to a CLI process shelling out directly to `mysqldump`/`mysql`/`tar`. New `wp yeffoprint migrate export`/`wp yeffoprint migrate import` commands (`includes/cli/class-migrate-command.php`), WP-CLI-only and never auto-run — same reasoning `class-seed-command.php` already documents for why a destructive/operational command like this has no HTTP trigger at all.

- **`export`** writes `database.sql.gz` (via `mysqldump --single-transaction`, piped straight to `gzip` so nothing is buffered in PHP), `uploads.tar.gz` (via `tar -C`, likewise streamed), and a small `manifest.json` (site URL, WP version, timestamp) into a fresh timestamped directory. That directory defaults to one level *above* the WordPress install root (`dirname(ABSPATH) . '/yp-migrations/'`) — deliberately outside both the web-servable docroot and the git working tree the deploy cron `git reset --hard`s every few minutes, since a backup carrying every customer's name/address/order history has no business anywhere web-accessible. `.htaccess`/`index.php` hardening is added regardless, in case `--output` is ever pointed somewhere that isn't.
- **`import`** takes an `export`-produced directory, confirms (destructively overwrites the DB and/or media) unless `--yes`, then reverses the process — `mysql < gunzip` for the database, `tar -x --strip-components=1` for uploads (with an opt-in `--clean-uploads` to fully replace rather than merge on top of whatever's already there).
- **Domain didn't need to change** (same domain, different server) — confirmed directly, so this deliberately does *not* implement its own serialization-safe search-replace of the site URL in the DB dump. WP-CLI already ships exactly that (`wp search-replace`, which correctly walks PHP-serialized values in `wp_options`/postmeta rather than naive string substitution, which corrupts them) — `import` just warns if the backup's recorded `site_url` doesn't match the current one, and points at that existing command rather than re-implementing it.
- **DB credentials never appear on the command line** (would sit in plain sight in `ps aux` for the command's whole runtime) — written instead to a temporary, `chmod 0600` `--defaults-extra-file`, deleted immediately after each mysqldump/mysql call.
- **`DB_HOST` parsing** mirrors what `wpdb` itself accepts — plain hostname, `host:port`, `host:/path/to/socket`, and an optional `p:` (persistent connection) prefix stripped before use — so a socket-based local DB connection doesn't silently get misread as a TCP hostname.

Verify (on the new server, after `export` on the old one and copying the output directory over): `wp yeffoprint migrate import <dir>`, then load the site and confirm both data (orders, products, settings) and media (images) are present and correct.

### Correction: a migration plugin already existed — `yeffoprint-migrate` — and this capability moved there
