**No admin-configurable ordering, on purpose.** Rotation order is just `YeffoPrint_Promo_Themes::all()`'s own fixed definition order (roughly seasonal/calendar order, with the always-on Web Design theme last), filtered down to whichever themes are actually active. The request was "select more than one," not "control the order" — adding a real reorder UI (drag-and-drop, up/down buttons, whatever) for a purely cosmetic ordering concern nobody asked for would have been the wrong trade against the two admin surfaces this already has to stay working on.

**Both admin surfaces get the plain version of the same idea, not a repeater.** With only 13 possible themes total, "one row per known theme, filled in or not" is simpler and fully sufficient — no add/remove/reorder repeater UI needed anywhere, since the full set of *possible* rows never changes. `render_promo_banners_field()` (the classic, unlinked-fallback Settings page, Phase 8) renders a plain `<table>` of 13 rows with nested `promo_banners[<slug>][offer]`/`[code]` inputs, no JS. `views/settings.js` (the admin-app, the actual UI staff use) renders the identical 13 rows via the existing `.yp-tier-table` styling already built for the bulk-discount-tier repeater, reading/writing `data-yp-promo-offer="<slug>"`/`data-yp-promo-code="<slug>"` inputs into the same `promo_banners` shape on save.

**One-time migration**, not a breaking change for a site that already had a single banner configured: `YeffoPrint_Admin_Menu::get_promo_banners()` checks `PROMO_BANNERS_OPTION` with `get_option()`'s `false` default (distinct from an explicitly saved empty array) as the one-shot guard — same "auto-create on first use" idiom `YeffoPrint_Pricing_Rule::get_active_rule_id()` already uses for its own option — and on a still-unmigrated site, seeds the new option from the old `PROMO_THEME_OPTION_LEGACY`/`PROMO_OFFER_OPTION_LEGACY`/`PROMO_CODE_OPTION_LEGACY` values (kept around only for this one read, no longer registered as settings or rendered anywhere) before returning. A site that never had a banner configured just migrates to an empty map, same as if it had never existed.

**Frontend**: `render.php` now loops `YeffoPrint_Admin_Menu::active_promo_banners()` instead of reading one theme/offer/code. With exactly one active theme it renders a single bare `.yp-promo` section — byte-for-byte the same shape the pre-rotation markup produced, no wrapper, no JS engaged — so the common case (most sites, most of the time, run one promo at a time) is completely unchanged. Two or more wraps every slide in `.yp-promo-rotator`; CSS (`patterns.css`) shows exactly one slide at a time via `display:none`/`.is-active` rather than an absolutely-positioned crossfade stack, since theme copy lengths vary slightly and this sidesteps needing a JS-measured shared height. `site.js`'s `initPromoRotator()` auto-advances every 7s, pauses on hover/keyboard focus (an uncontrollable countdown is a real accessibility problem, not a nicety), skips the timer entirely under `prefers-reduced-motion` (manual dot clicks still work), and restarts the countdown fresh after a manual dot pick rather than auto-advancing away from it moments later. Dot indicators sit on a translucent dark pill rather than theme-colored — the 13 themes range from near-black to near-white backgrounds, so a single fixed dot color would disappear against roughly half of them; the pill guarantees contrast against any of them.

Verify: fill in Offer + Promo code for two different themes (e.g. "Summer" and "Web Design Launch") and enable → confirm the homepage shows a rotating banner, auto-advancing every ~7s, with dot indicators at the bottom → click a dot → confirm it jumps to that banner and the countdown restarts rather than immediately auto-advancing again → hover over the banner → confirm auto-advance pauses, and resumes on mouse-leave → tab into the banner's CTA link via keyboard → confirm auto-advance pauses the same way → clear back down to exactly one active theme → confirm the banner renders as a single bare section again with no dots and no rotation → clear all themes back out (or disable) → confirm nothing renders → on a site with a pre-existing single-banner selection from before this change, confirm it appears correctly as that one theme's active banner after the migration runs.

## Fix: the homepage hero's press-proof graphic read as oddly small and narrow on mobile (direct report, with a phone screenshot: "can it be squared off more and a bit bigger to take up more space")

`.yp-proof`, the white card in `patterns/hero.php`, was already a perfect square (`aspect-ratio: 1/1`) at every viewport — the actual problem was what sat inside it. The SVG mark (`.yp-proof__mark`) was rendered at only `width: 42%` of the card, and its own shape (`viewBox="0 0 120 136"`, three CMY bars clipped to a rounded path roughly 88×128) was a tall, narrow pill rather than anything square — so on a phone, where this card is the most prominent thing below the fold-adjacent hero copy, it read as a large mostly-empty white square with a thin colored strip pinned in the middle.

Two independent changes, matching the two-part request. **"Squared off"**: `hero.php`'s clip shape is now a plain `<rect width="104" height="104" rx="16">` on a `120×120` viewBox instead of a hand-drawn asymmetric-radius path on a `120×136` one — the three bars are equal-width squares of the card's own aspect ratio now, not a portrait pill. **"Bigger, take up more space"**: `.yp-proof__mark`'s width went from `42%` to `62%` of the card. Both numbers were chosen so the mark still clears `.yp-proof__corner`'s fixed 16px inset brackets and the bottom `.yp-proof__caption` at every size this card renders at (mobile through desktop, since the card's own `max-width: 400px` and `aspect-ratio: 1/1` didn't change) — verified against the card's smallest realistic width, not just its 400px ceiling.

Scoped to the homepage hero only: `class-promo-themes.php`'s promo-banner reuse of this visual motif is a CSS-independent implementation (`.yp-promo__proof`, not `.yp-proof`) per its own docblock, so it renders unchanged.

Verify: load the homepage on a phone-width viewport → confirm the press-proof card's colored mark now fills noticeably more of the white card and reads as a square swatch rather than a narrow pill, with clear white margin still visible around it (not touching the corner brackets or the caption text) → check desktop width → confirm the same, proportionally, since nothing here is breakpoint-gated → confirm the color-snap-in entrance animation still plays correctly with the new shape.

## Manual order creation: waive the design fee, and stop forcing an unpaid order straight to "Processing" (direct report, after creating a manual order: "I need the ability to waive the design fee in some cases. Also, it went straight to processing status, even though it hadn't been paid for. Should be placed in pending payment status. Also the payment link the woocommerce order screen gives to pay gives an error that the order can't be paid for")

**Bug: manual creation forced the order to `processing` regardless of payment.** `YeffoPrint_Manual_Order_Creator::create()` ended with an unconditional `$order->update_status( 'processing', ... )`, added back in Phase A only to make the linked `yp_custom_order` shell publish/link for free via the existing `woocommerce_order_status_processing` hook (`class-custom-order-payment.php::link_paid_custom_orders()`). That directly caused both symptoms in the report: WooCommerce treats `processing` as "already paid," so (a) the Dashboard/order screen showed a not-yet-paid order as if it were already in production, and (b) `WC_Order::needs_payment()` returns false once an order is `processing`, which is exactly why the order-pay link WooCommerce's own order screen offers threw "This order cannot be paid for" — the order looked, to WooCommerce, like there was nothing left to pay.

The fix removes that forced transition entirely. The order now stays at `pending` — the same status `wc_create_order()` already gives it two lines earlier, and the same "pending payment" status a real storefront checkout starts an order in. `YeffoPrint_Custom_Order_Meta::create_shell()`'s own docblock already documented the intended rule ("stays 'draft' until a linked WC order line item actually reaches 'processing' — same rule regardless of origin") — the manual-order path was the one place not actually following it. No change was needed to the linking logic itself: once the order is genuinely paid (the order-pay link, a manual status change, whatever gateway staff use), `class-custom-order-payment.php`'s existing hooks fire exactly as they do for a normal checkout.

**New: a "Waive the design fee" checkbox on the Custom Design tab of the manual-order screen** (`views/manual-order.js`), staff-only — there's no equivalent on the customer-facing form, a customer can't waive their own fee. Reuses the exact skip-the-fee shape the codebase already had for customer-provided designs and fee-free reorders (`YeffoPrint_Custom_Order_Meta::is_fee_skipped()`), rather than inventing a parallel one: a new `FEE_WAIVED` meta flag joins `CUSTOMER_PROVIDED_DESIGN`/`SOURCE_CUSTOM_ORDER_ID` in that check, kept as its own distinct flag (not a reuse of `CUSTOMER_PROVIDED_DESIGN`) because the two mean different things — "no design work needed" isn't true when staff have simply chosen not to charge for design work that still has to happen. `YeffoPrint_Manual_Order_Creator::add_custom_design_rows()` skips adding the fee line item and sets the new meta instead when the checkbox is checked; everything downstream (`find_design_fee()` recording $0, the classic Custom Order editor's "Submission" row and a dedicated staff-facing notice, the admin-app order detail's existing `fee_skipped` display, `class-reorder.php`'s reorder-link rendering) already reads through `is_fee_skipped()`/the fee-item's mere absence, so all of it picked this up with no further changes. The price preview on the manual-order screen switches to the existing `own_design` pricing-preview mode when the checkbox is checked, so staff see the real $0-design-fee total before submitting, not a total that's about to change.

Verify: create a manual Custom Design order without checking the new box → confirm the order is created at "Pending payment" (not Processing) → open the WooCommerce order screen's order-pay/payment link → confirm it now works (no "cannot be paid for" error) → actually pay it (or move it to Processing manually) → confirm the linked custom order shell publishes and the $25 design fee shows correctly. Then create another manual Custom Design order with "Waive the design fee" checked → confirm the price preview shows $0 design fee before submitting → confirm the created order has no design-fee line item → confirm the Custom Order editor shows the "fee waived by staff" notice and "New design (fee waived by staff)" in Submission → pay the order → confirm the linked shell records a $0 design fee, not the $25 default.

## Fix: "Add Proof" failed with "No route was found matching the URL and request method" (direct report, from the Custom Order screen)

`views/orders.js`'s `addProof()` and `views/proofs.js`'s own `save()` both POSTed to `endpoint( 'proof' )` — resolving to `admin/proof`, singular. `class-admin-proof-controller.php` never registered that route for `POST`: it registers `POST /admin/proofs` (plural, `create_proof()`) and, separately, `DELETE /admin/proof/{id}` (singular, an existing proof by ID — `proofs.js`'s own delete button already used this one correctly). A bare `POST /admin/proof` matches neither, so WordPress's REST router had nothing to dispatch it to — exactly the raw "No route was found" error text, not a permission or validation failure from the controller itself (the request never reached it). Both call sites now POST to `endpoint( 'proofs' )`, matching the registered route; the DELETE call, which was already correct, is unchanged.

Verify: from the Custom Orders screen, open a paid order and click "Add Proof" → pick a file from the media library → confirm it uploads successfully and appears in the Proofs list (no "No route was found" error) → separately, from the standalone Proofs screen, click "+ Add Proof," pick a Custom Order and a file → confirm that succeeds too → confirm deleting a proof from the Proofs screen still works (that call was never broken).

## Manual order creation: email the customer their order details + payment link on creation (direct request: "when I manually create the order, can you draft up an email template that will send the customer an email with their order details as well as the link to pay?")

Reuses WooCommerce's own built-in "Order details" email (`WC_Email_Customer_Invoice`, historically called the Customer Invoice email — `manual = true`, the exact one core's own "Email invoice / order details to customer" order action already sends) rather than building a parallel one: `YeffoPrint_Manual_Order_Creator::create()` now calls `WC()->mailer()->customer_invoice( $order )` right after the order is created, when the new "Email the customer their order details and a payment link" checkbox (`views/manual-order.js`, checked by default, all three order types) is checked. This class's `trigger()` doesn't gate on the email's admin-configurable "enabled" setting — it's designed for on-demand sending, same as core's own order-action button — so this fires reliably regardless of that toggle.

The template itself needed no changes: this theme already has its own copy of it (`woocommerce/emails/customer-invoice.php`, a pre-existing override — see that file's own docblock), and it already does exactly what was asked — an order items/totals table via the shared `woocommerce_email_order_details` hook, and a real payment link (`$order->get_checkout_payment_url()`) whenever `$order->needs_payment()` is true, which a freshly-created manual order always is now that it's left at "Pending payment" (see the fix above this one). A waived design fee or a paid-in-full $0 order is reflected automatically too, with no extra plumbing — the template already branches on the order's real state.

This is also the fix for a separately-reported "your cart is empty" error a customer hit clicking "the link to pay": that phrasing points at WooCommerce's plain checkout page (`WC_Shortcode_Checkout::checkout()`, which requires cart items) rather than its order-pay endpoint (`order_pay()`, which needs none — it works off the order directly) — meaning whatever link was actually clicked wasn't built from `$order->get_checkout_payment_url()` the way this email's link now always is. Subject line, heading, and the "additional content" footer text are all staff-editable in WooCommerce → Settings → Emails → Order details, same as any other WooCommerce email — no code changes needed there.

Verify: create a manual order with the new checkbox checked → confirm the customer receives an email with the order's items/total and a "Pay for this order" link → click that link while logged in as that customer → confirm it lands on a real payment page for that order (not "your cart is empty") → pay it → confirm the order updates normally. Create another manual order with the checkbox unchecked → confirm no email is sent. Create one with "Waive the design fee" also checked → confirm the emailed order total excludes the design fee.

## Fix: the "Pay for order" page rendered with none of the site's WooCommerce styling at all (direct report, with a screenshot: plain browser radio buttons, square table, an unstyled "Pay with Venmo" text link — "make it a lot nicer, make it match the style of the site, fix the ugly buttons")

Root cause was the theme's own `assets/css/woocommerce.css` never loading on this page — not a CSS specificity loss (the file already has a whole section built for exactly this page, `.wc_payment_methods`/`#order_review`/`#place_order`, some already `!important`-guarded), which the screenshot itself ruled out: not even an ID-selector `!important` rule was landing, and neither was the plain `.woocommerce a.button` rule on the "Pay with Venmo" link. `functions.php`'s enqueue gate for this stylesheet checked `is_cart() || is_checkout() || is_account_page()` — `checkout/order-pay/{id}` (reached from a declined-payment retry link or an admin-created order's own payment link — `class-manual-order-creator.php`'s new invoice email above included) is technically still "the Checkout page" as far as most WooCommerce internals are concerned, but not reliably enough to trust blind given the visual evidence. Fixed by also checking `is_wc_endpoint_url( 'order-pay' )`/`'order-received'` directly in that gate — the same check WooCommerce's own `Checkout.php::is_checkout_endpoint()` uses internally to decide whether to fall back to this exact classic template in the first place.

With the stylesheet actually loading, the section already built for this page does most of the work: the classic payment-method list (`.wc_payment_methods`) gets full card borders and a custom-drawn radio to match the Cart/Checkout blocks' own radio styling, `#order_review` gets a card treatment, and `table.shop_table` gets the same rounded/shadowed look as every other table on the site. A few more targeted upgrades on top, matching "fix the ugly buttons" directly: `#place_order` (this page's own submit button, which only ever renders here — the real Cart/Checkout blocks have their own separate submit button, styled independently) now gets the same accent-gradient CTA treatment as every other primary "commit to paying" button on the site (login's `#wp-submit`, `.is-style-accent` elsewhere) instead of the flatter near-black every other plain `.button` gets. `#order_review` also picked up a max-width and its own bottom-margin on the table inside, since on this one page it wraps the *entire* page's content (table, payment methods, submit button) rather than just a right-column summary the way that same ID means on a normal two-column checkout — it needed room to read as the page's one content card, not a cramped sidebar. Every rule in this classic-only section now also carries `!important`, matching the convention the Blocks section above it already established, as insurance against any further specificity surprises.

Verify: open a "Pay for order" link (a declined Venmo/Zelle payment's retry link, or a manually-created order's payment link/email) while logged out and while logged in as that order's own customer → confirm the page now has real page padding, a card-wrapped order table matching the rest of the site, a bordered/radio-styled payment method list (including the unselected options, not just whichever one starts selected), and a gradient "Pay for order" button matching the site's other primary CTAs → confirm the normal Cart/Checkout pages (which render via the Blocks, not this classic template) are visually unchanged.

### Follow-up: a real two-column layout, a page heading, and the actual root cause of the "weird box" (direct report with a fresh screenshot, taken after the fix above: "still looks awful... no title, just blank space... everything centered down the middle... weird box around the payment methods... Pay with Venmo button looks wrong")

The screenshot showed the exact same symptoms as before this file's fix (unstyled radio buttons, no card on the table, "Pay with Venmo" as bare text) — `yeffoprint_asset_version()`'s own content-hash cache-busting (this file's own top-of-file comment) rules out a stale-CSS-file read, so rather than assume the deploy just hadn't landed yet, this pass re-verified every claim against WooCommerce's actual vendored source instead of trusting the previous pass's reasoning. That surfaced a real, separate bug the previous fix missed: WooCommerce's own default frontend stylesheet (`wp-content/plugins/woocommerce/assets/css/woocommerce.css`, registered as the `woocommerce-general` handle — always enqueued, independent of theme, nothing to do with the block-theme-only `woocommerce-blocktheme.css` this file's own comments already covered) sets `#payment { background: rgba(129,110,153,.14); border-radius: 5px }` and `#payment div.payment_box { background-color: #dcd7e2 }` — a flat lavender-purple, not a color this site's palette produces anywhere. Neither selector was ever addressed, so that background painted straight through regardless of whether this theme's own stylesheet loaded correctly.

**Two-column layout** (direct request): `#order_review`'s only two direct children in `checkout/form-pay.php` are `table.shop_table` and `#payment` — turning `#order_review` into a two-column CSS grid (stacking to one column under 782px) makes them the two columns with no template override needed, order summary on the left and the payment method card on the right, matching how a real checkout reads rather than one undifferentiated stacked block.

**The "weird box"**: `#order_review #payment` now gets this site's own card treatment (white background, radius, shadow) in place of WooCommerce's unbranded lavender default — the fix for the root cause found above, not just a wash of `!important` over symptoms.

**"Pay with Venmo button looks wrong"**: it's a real `<a class="button">` (`class-manual-payment-gateway.php`'s `payment_action_html()`), so it already inherits this file's `.woocommerce a.button` treatment by class alone — added its own display/spacing rule so it reads as a distinct button inside the tighter `.payment_box` padding rather than running into the paragraph text above it. Also added `-webkit-appearance: none` alongside the existing unprefixed `appearance: none` on the custom radio circles, belt-and-suspenders for older WebKit.

**No title, floating content**: `page-checkout.html` has no page-title block at all by design — the normal Checkout Blocks flow never needed one. This is the one page reached cold (from an email or a retry link) that does. `functions.php` now prepends a "Complete Your Payment" heading via a `the_content` filter gated tightly to `is_checkout_pay_page()`, so every other page — including the normal Checkout page itself — is untouched.

Verify: open a "Pay for order" link → confirm a "Complete Your Payment" heading now appears above the content → confirm the order table (left) and payment method card (right) render as two distinct columns on desktop, stacking to one column on mobile → confirm the payment card's background is now a real white/shadowed card, not a lavender tint → confirm "Pay with Venmo" (or Zelle) renders as an actual button with its own spacing, not bare underlined text → confirm the normal Checkout page (Blocks-based) is unaffected by all of the above.

### Follow-up: three more `!important` gaps, found from a real screenshot after the layout landed correctly (direct report: "weird arrow above each payment method that runs into the title... Pay with Venmo button still looks bad... for the card method, I have to actually hit the word card")

The layout fix above landed correctly (screenshot confirmed the heading, two columns, and the gradient submit button all rendering right) — these three remaining issues were the same class of bug as everything else in this file's history, just three rules that still lacked `!important` where a competing, more-specific selector was quietly winning:

1. **The stray arrow**: `.wc_payment_methods .payment_box::before` (hides WooCommerce's own css-triangle "speech bubble" pointer) never had `!important`. WooCommerce's own rule for that same triangle uses an ID selector (`#payment div.payment_box::before`) — higher specificity than this plain two-class one — so it kept winning and the arrow kept rendering, running into the payment method's title exactly as reported.
2. **"Pay with Venmo" looking bad**: the shared `.woocommerce a.button` rule (also used by `#place_order` and every other classic button) never declared `padding` or `display` at all — theme.json's generated global-styles stylesheet only reaches real Gutenberg `.wp-block-button__link` elements, never a bare classic `<a class="button">`, so this link had a background color and nothing else: no padding, shrink-wrapped to its own text. Added the same padding `.wp-block-button__link` uses (global.css) plus `display: inline-block`.
3. **Only the word "Card" was clickable**: Venmo/Zelle (this codebase's own gateway, `class-manual-payment-gateway.php`, with no CSS of its own competing for this selector) worked fine; the real card-payment gateway plugin ships its own CSS for this same `<label>`, apparently more specific than `.wc_payment_methods .wc_payment_method > label`'s plain two-class selector — `display: block` (also missing `!important`) kept losing, leaving the label at whatever inline/inline-block default the gateway's own CSS set, shrink-wrapped to just the label text instead of the full clickable row. Added `!important` plus an explicit `width: 100%` so this holds regardless of what any given gateway's own CSS does.

Verify: open a "Pay for order" link with at least Card, Venmo, and Zelle all enabled → confirm no stray triangle/arrow appears above any option's title, selected or not → confirm "Pay with Venmo"/"Pay with Zelle" render as properly padded buttons, not cramped text → confirm clicking anywhere in the Card row (not just the word "Card") selects that payment method, same as Venmo/Zelle already did.

## Fix: Venmo/Zelle Apps Script silently swallowed unparseable payment emails (direct report, after a domain change: "payment matching isn't matching the payment... it applied the labels to it... but the site never received the request")

Live-debugged against the actual production endpoint (`curl`-testing `https://yeffodesign.com/wp-json/yeffoprint-core/v1/payments/notify` directly, including with the real webhook URL/token the user's Apps Script uses) — the route, token, and request format were all confirmed working (`{"status":"unmatched"}`, HTTP 200), ruling out the domain change, `class-payment-webhook-controller.php`, and `class-payment-webhook-secret.php` entirely. The domain change was a coincidence, not the cause.

The real bug was in `docs/venmo-zelle-setup.md`'s own Apps Script template (Step 3): when a payment email's body didn't match the `amountMatch` regex, the script labeled the thread `-Processed` (so it looks handled) and returned — with **no log entry at all**, before the code path that calls `UrlFetchApp.fetch()` and `Logger.log()`s the response. That's indistinguishable, from the Execution log alone, from a real webhook problem: both show a clean "Execution completed" with nothing useful in it. Fixed by logging the specific email's subject and a clear explanation the moment the regex fails to match, so this failure mode is now visible instead of silent — same fix needed in-place for anyone already running an older copy of this script (see that file's own new troubleshooting note).

Verify: temporarily break the regex against a real payment email (or find one that already doesn't match your account's exact Venmo/Zelle wording) → run `checkForPayments` by hand → confirm the Execution log now names the specific email and says a dollar amount couldn't be found, rather than logging nothing.

### Follow-up: the "unmatched" admin notification email showed `&#36;227.30` instead of `$227.30`

Confirmed cosmetic, not a matching bug — `on_hold_orders_by_amount()`/`order_from_note()` compare the raw `$amount` float against `$order->get_total()` directly, never touching the formatted display string, so this never affected which order (if any) got matched. `wc_price( $amount )` returns HTML with the currency symbol as an entity (`&#36;` for `$`), and `wp_strip_all_tags()` only strips tags, not entities — fine when the result lands somewhere HTML renders it (the order note `mark_paid()` writes, shown in wp-admin), but these particular strings land in a plain-text `wp_mail()` body, which has no HTML decoding to fall back on. Added a small `plain_text_amount()` helper (`html_entity_decode()` on top of the existing `wp_strip_all_tags( wc_price( $amount ) )`) and used it at the three admin-notification-email call sites only — `mark_paid()`'s own order-note usage is untouched, since decoding there would be wrong (it needs the entity, not a raw `$`, for its HTML rendering context).

Verify: trigger an "unmatched" or "ambiguous" admin notification (a payment amount with no on-hold order, or two on-hold orders sharing an exact total) → confirm the email now reads `$227.30`, not `&#36;227.30`.

## Admin panel: cosmetic rename to YeffoDesign, a Pending Orders detail drawer, and a re-worked Settings screen (direct request, three parts)

**Part A — rename.** "YeffoPrint" → "YeffoDesign" in the admin panel's *visible* UI only, per explicit instruction not to touch scripts, class names, option keys, or slugs: `app.js`'s sidebar wordmark and `class-admin-menu.php`'s `add_menu_page()` title/menu labels (its unreachable, unused `render_dashboard()` method's stray `<h1>` was also updated for consistency, though nothing routes to it). The `'yeffoprint'` menu slug, every `YeffoPrint_*` class name, and every `yeffoprint-core`/`yeffoprint_*` option key and REST namespace are unchanged.

**Part B — Pending Orders detail drawer.** Direct request: "I'd like to be able to click on a row and see the details of the order... in that popout sidebar similar to the custom orders so I don't need to switch to the woocommerce orders screen." New `class-admin-order-controller.php` endpoints — `GET`/`EDITABLE /admin/order/{id}` alongside the existing `POST /admin/order/{id}/send-to-printer` — return a `detail_payload()` (customer, shipping address, payment method, line items, totals, `edit_url`) built entirely from `WC_Order`'s own accessors, with `item_payload()` reusing `WC_Order_Item::get_formatted_meta_data()` — the exact same call the classic order screen's own item table already renders customizations/batch tables/QR links with, so nothing about that display logic needed reimplementing. `status_options()` reads `wc_get_order_statuses()` directly rather than hand-maintaining a duplicate list, so this plugin's own custom statuses ("In Production," "Shipped") are automatically included. `app.js`'s Pending Orders panel row now opens a new drawer (`openWcOrderDrawer()`/`loadWcOrderDetail()`/`renderWcOrderDetail()`, modeled directly on `orders.js`'s own `openDetail()`/`loadDetail()`/`renderDetail()`) showing all of the above plus a status `<select>` + Save button (`saveWcOrderStatus()`, POSTing to the same endpoint) and an "Open in WooCommerce" escape hatch (`edit_url`) for anything this drawer doesn't cover (refunds, notes, editing line items) — reusing the existing `.yp-drawer`/`.yp-record-table`/`.yp-panel` classes, no new CSS needed. `dashboardSectionHtml()` gained an optional `clickAttr` parameter (defaulting to the pre-existing `data-yp-dashboard-order` attribute) so Pending Orders' rows could get their own click-target attribute (`data-yp-wc-order`) distinct from the Custom Orders panels that already used the generic one to hash-navigate instead.

**Part C — Settings re-worked into tabs.** Direct report: "settings needs to be re-worked. It's becoming too cluttered." `views/settings.js`'s ~10 panels (Announcement Bar, Homepage Promo, Label Configurator, Shipment Tracking, Contact Form, Splash Screen, Dashboard, Telegram Bot, Social Login, Maintenance Subscription) were all rendering on one long scroll. Grouped into four tabs — **General** (Announcement Bar, Splash Screen, Dashboard due-date), **Storefront** (Homepage Promo, Label Configurator, Contact Form), **Shipping** (UPS/USPS tracking credentials), **Integrations** (Telegram Bot, Social Login, Maintenance Subscription) — via a small `.yp-settings-tabs` pill nav (new CSS in `records.css`) and the native `hidden` attribute toggled per tab section. This is presentation-only: every panel's markup, and therefore every field id `save()` reads, still renders into the DOM on load regardless of which tab is active, so `class-admin-settings-controller.php`'s single comprehensive `GET`/`POST /admin/settings` pair (deliberately one endpoint per that file's own docblock — "a single Save on this screen... should save all of them together") and `views/settings.js`'s single Save button are both unchanged. `render()` takes an optional `activeTabId` so a Save keeps the same tab open afterward instead of resetting to General.

Verify: open the admin panel → confirm the sidebar reads "YeffoDesign" and the browser's wp-admin menu label does too, with the URL slug (`admin.php?page=yeffoprint`) unchanged. From the Dashboard, click a Pending Orders row → confirm a drawer opens with that order's customer, shipping address, payment method, every line item's full customizations/quantities, and totals → change its status and Save → confirm it updates and the row disappears from Pending Orders once it's no longer "Processing" → click "Open in WooCommerce" → confirm it lands on that order's classic edit screen. Open Settings → confirm four tabs (General/Storefront/Shipping/Integrations) each show a subset of the previous panels → change a field on one tab, switch tabs, switch back → confirm the value is still there → Save → confirm the active tab stays open and every field across all four tabs is still persisted correctly (spot-check a field from a tab that wasn't active during the save).

## Pending Orders drawer: print a real shipping label without leaving the drawer (direct request: "can we integrate the woocommerce shipping plugin already active so I can print labels right from there? Without having to go to woocommerce?")

This store has the official **WooCommerce Shipping** plugin active (`wp-content/plugins/woocommerce-shipping`, `Automattic\WCShipping\Loader`) — it purchases and prints real USPS/UPS/DHL labels. That plugin has no public REST API of its own for driving rate-shopping/label purchase from outside its UI; it only ever renders that whole flow (a large proprietary React app — package dimensions, rate shopping, customs forms, payment, printing) as a single meta box, `#woocommerce-order-label`, registered on the classic order edit screen (`Loader::add_order_meta_boxes()`). Reimplementing that logic against its ~20 internal REST controllers (`LabelPurchaseRESTController`, `LabelRateRESTController`, `LabelPrintController`, etc.) would mean duplicating a commercial plugin's entire purchase/print flow — high effort, high risk, and it'd drift out of sync with every future WooCommerce Shipping update.

Instead, `class-admin-order-controller.php`'s `detail_payload()` gained one flag, `shipping_label_available` (`class_exists( '\Automattic\WCShipping\Loader' )`, so the panel simply doesn't render on a store without this plugin active), and `app.js`'s WC order drawer (`wcOrderShippingLabelHtml()`) got a new "Shipping Label" panel with a "Print Shipping Label" button. Clicking it (`embedShippingLabel()`) inserts a same-origin `<iframe src="{edit_url}#woocommerce-order-label">` — the real classic order edit screen, loaded invisibly inside the drawer — and, once it loads, injects a `<style>` element directly into the iframe's own document (same-origin, so this is plain DOM access, no special API) that hides every other element on that page and leaves only the `#woocommerce-order-label` meta box visible: WordPress core's admin bar/sidebar/footer (`#wpadminbar`, `#adminmenumain`, `#wpfooter`), WooCommerce core's own order-data box (`#woocommerce-order-data`), and every *other* meta box via the generic `#poststuff .postbox:not(#woocommerce-order-label)` rule — nothing in that CSS is specific to WooCommerce Shipping's own internal markup, which this never touches or depends on staying stable. What renders is that plugin's real, fully-functional label form — actual rates from actual carrier accounts, actual label purchase and PDF printing — running its own unmodified code, just visually isolated inside this drawer. The existing "Open in WooCommerce" link at the bottom of the drawer (already there before this change) doubles as a fallback if the iframe ever fails to load for some reason (the CSS-injection step is wrapped in try/catch for exactly that case).

Verify: open a Pending Order that has at least one shippable line item → confirm a "Shipping Label" panel appears with a "Print Shipping Label" button → click it → confirm the real WooCommerce Shipping label form appears inline in the drawer (not the surrounding wp-admin chrome) → purchase and print a label → confirm it appears normally in WooCommerce's own order screen afterward (this embed doesn't change how or where labels are stored). On a store without WooCommerce Shipping active, confirm the panel doesn't render at all.

## Fix: reordering didn't work on Web Design Packages (and the same latent bug in Materials, Sizes, Sticker Sizes) — direct report: "I can't seem to reorder the web design packages, the button is there, I can click it, but it doesn't actually move"

Root cause: each of these four admin-app screens' `move()` reorders by **swapping** the clicked record's `menu_order` with its neighbor's — but `save()` never set `menu_order` when *creating* a record, so every record added through any of these screens (as opposed to the one-time `wp yeffoprint setup-web-design-packages` CLI seed, which does set it) defaulted to `menu_order` 0. Swapping two values that are already equal is a no-op: the two `POST` requests both succeed (no error surfaces), but nothing changes — exactly the reported symptom. Any store where these screens were used to add records by hand, rather than seeded, was already affected; Web Design Packages simply has no CLI seeder giving it a head start, so it was the first to surface it.

Fixed two ways, in `views/web-design-packages.js`, `views/materials.js`, `views/sizes.js`, and `views/sticker-sizes.js` alike:
1. **`move()` now renumbers the whole list** (every record's `menu_order` set to its own array index in the new order) instead of swapping two values — this can't tie the way a pairwise swap can, and it's self-healing: the moment any record in an already-tied list is moved, every record in that list gets a distinct, correct `menu_order`, no separate data migration needed.
2. **`save()` now sets `menu_order` to the current list length when creating** a new record, so it's appended at the end instead of defaulting to 0 (which would otherwise have sorted a brand-new record to the very top of the list, and reintroduced the original tie for the next record added after it).

Verify: on Web Design Packages (or Materials/Sizes/Sticker Sizes), click ↑/↓ on a row → confirm it visibly moves and the list stays in that order after a reload → add a new record → confirm it appears at the bottom of the list, not the top → reorder it → confirm that works immediately too.

## Shipping Label panel: surface the customer's chosen shipping method (direct question: "is there a way... it auto selects the shipping method for me based on what the customer selected?")

Investigated and declined true auto-selection. WooCommerce Shipping only remembers a rate/package well enough to pre-fill the label form when its *own* live carrier-rate shopping was the shipping method used at checkout (it stores that exact rate/package data as `wcshipping_packages` order-item meta — `class-wc-connect-order-presenter.php`/`View.php` both read it). This store's 3 shipping options (USPS Ground Advantage, UPS 2nd Day Air, USPS First Class International) are plain WooCommerce shipping methods, not that plugin's live-rate method, so there's no order data linking them to a specific carrier service — and the plugin has no settings screen to map one to the other. The only way to actually auto-select a rate would be a script reaching into WooCommerce Shipping's live-rendered React form and clicking whichever option's text happens to match — undocumented, unversioned internal markup that could silently start clicking the wrong (paid) service after any plugin update. Declined for that reason, with the user's agreement.

Instead, `class-admin-order-controller.php`'s `detail_payload()` gained `shipping_method` (`$order->get_shipping_method()` — the same accessor WooCommerce Shipping's own order presenter already reads), and `app.js`'s `wcOrderShippingLabelHtml()` now shows "Customer selected: **{method}**" directly above the embedded label form. It doesn't select anything automatically, but it puts the answer exactly where staff are about to pick a rate — a one-glance match instead of checking a separate screen, with full manual control preserved for when a different service is actually needed.

Verify: open a Pending Order with a recorded shipping method → confirm "Customer selected: {method}" appears above the "Print Shipping Label" button, matching what the customer actually chose at checkout.

## Fix: Shipping Label panel stayed hidden on Pending Orders despite the plugin being active (direct report: "I don't see the shipping option... under the pending orders")

Long diagnostic chain, since the symptom looked identical to several other explanations that all turned out to be wrong: first ruled out deploy (`Prod` on GitHub had the code — confirmed via the GitHub API), then the auto-deploy pipeline itself (this store deploys via a cron job pulling every 5 minutes, not the repo's separate GitHub Actions SSH workflow — that workflow has in fact been failing on every run since it was created, `missing server host`, because its `GCE_HOST`/`GCE_USER`/`GCE_SSH_PRIVATE_KEY` secrets were never added in GitHub, but that's a dead/unused path here, not what's actually deploying this site), then PHP opcache (ruled out — a later fix, the reorder-buttons one, was already showing live, so PHP was serving fresh code). Checking the actual REST response in the browser's Network tab settled it: `shipping_label_available` was `false`, even though WooCommerce Shipping showed Active in wp-admin and its meta box rendered fine on the classic order screen.

Root cause: `detail_payload()`'s check, `class_exists( '\Automattic\WCShipping\Loader' )`, depends on one specific version's internal namespaced class name — version-fragile in a way this environment's own copy of the plugin (used to write and verify that check) apparently doesn't share with whatever version is actually installed live. "The plugin is active" and "this exact internal class exists" turned out to be two different questions, and this store's real answer split them. Fixed by asking WordPress directly instead, the same way the Plugins screen itself determines Active/Inactive: `is_plugin_active( 'woocommerce-shipping/woocommerce-shipping.php' )` (new `is_shipping_plugin_active()` helper, with a `require_once ABSPATH . 'wp-admin/includes/plugin.php'` guard since that function isn't autoloaded outside `/wp-admin/` the way it is during a normal admin page load) — version-independent, and it's checking the exact same option data the "Active" label the user already confirmed is reading from.

Verify: open a Pending Order with a shippable line item → confirm the "Shipping Label" panel now appears (check the same order's REST response in DevTools if in doubt — `shipping_label_available` should now read `true`) → confirm "Print Shipping Label" still embeds the real form correctly.

## Fix: "Send to Printer" made a Pending Orders row disappear entirely (direct report: "I need pending orders to show processing and in production orders")

Root cause: `class-admin-dashboard-controller.php`'s `pending_wc_orders()` only ever queried `status: 'processing'` — the intended pipeline is Processing → In Production → Shipped (`class-order-production-status.php`'s own docblock), but "Send to Printer" moves an order to In Production, and this panel's query never included that status, so the row simply vanished from the one place staff use to track what's still in the pipeline before shipping.

Fixed by querying both statuses (`wc_get_orders( [ 'status' => [ 'processing', YeffoPrint_Order_Production_Status::STATUS ] ] )`) and returning each row's own `status`/`status_label`. `app.js`'s `sendToPrinterButtonHtml()` now checks that per row: still Processing → the button, same as before; already In Production → a status pill in the same column instead (no point offering the button again — `send_to_printer()`'s own 409 guard already refuses a second send, this just avoids showing a button that would error). `dashboardSectionHtml()` gained an optional `actionHeader` param (defaulting to blank, matching every other call site unchanged) so this one column could be labeled "Status" now that it's not purely an action button anymore.

Verify: click "Send to Printer" on a Processing order → confirm the row stays on the Pending Orders panel afterward, now showing an "In Production" pill instead of the button → confirm a still-Processing order elsewhere in the same panel still shows its Send to Printer button → confirm the row finally disappears once the order actually ships (moves past In Production).

## Pending Orders dashboard: one-click "Print Shipping Label" for In Production rows (direct follow-up: "the button to print the shipping label using woocommerce shipping")

Follow-up to the previous fix — once an In Production row stopped disappearing, its status pill alone didn't give staff the one-click convenience the Processing rows already had with "Send to Printer." Direct request: the same convenience for the next pipeline step, printing the actual shipping label, without opening the drawer first.

`class-admin-dashboard-controller.php`'s `get_summary()` gained a site-wide `shipping_label_available` flag (same `is_plugin_active( 'woocommerce-shipping/woocommerce-shipping.php' )` check as `class-admin-order-controller.php`'s own per-order use of it — duplicated rather than shared, matching this file's existing `orders_list_url()`/`YeffoPrint_Dashboard_Widgets::orders_list_url()` precedent). `app.js`'s `sendToPrinterButtonHtml()` now checks it for an In Production row: when the plugin's active, the row gets a real "Print Shipping Label" button instead of a plain pill; when it isn't, the pill stays as the honest fallback.

Rather than duplicate any drawer/embed logic for this dashboard-only shortcut, clicking that button calls the exact same `openWcOrderDrawer()` a row click already uses, with a new `autoPrintLabel` parameter threaded through `loadWcOrderDetail()` → `renderWcOrderDetail()`: once the drawer's own "Shipping Label" panel renders, it immediately calls the same `embedShippingLabel()` its own "Print Shipping Label" button would — skipping the one extra click inside the drawer, not a different code path from what already existed.

Verify: send an order to the printer so it's In Production → confirm the Pending Orders panel now shows a "Print Shipping Label" button in that row (not just a status pill) → click it → confirm the drawer opens and the WooCommerce Shipping form is already embedded, without needing a second click inside the drawer.

## Fix: printing a shipping label marked the order Completed instead of Shipped (direct report: "When I print a shipping label, it should change the status to shipped, but instead it marked it completed")

Two separate bugs, both in `maybe_advance_to_shipped()` (`class-order-shipment-status.php`):

1. **It had gone silent for the current pipeline.** It only ever fired when the order was currently "processing" — but that guard predates "Send to Printer" (`class-order-production-status.php`), which moves an order to "In Production" *before* staff ever gets to the label-purchase step. By the time a label actually exists, the order was never "processing" anymore, so this never ran at all in the real Processing → In Production → print-label flow.
2. **WooCommerce Shipping's own label-purchase form has its own "After purchasing a label, mark this order as complete and notify the customer" option** (confirmed in its compiled JS — not anything this plugin renders or controls). When that's checked, it calls WooCommerce's native complete-order action directly, landing the order on "completed" without ever passing through this store's own "shipped" status.

Fixed both by widening the starting-status check to `[ 'processing', YeffoPrint_Order_Production_Status::STATUS, 'completed' ]` instead of just `'processing'`. Accepting "in-production" restores the intended automatic advance for the real current pipeline; accepting "completed" means the moment a *new* (not-yet-recorded) shipment fingerprint shows up on an order that jumped straight to "completed", this redirects it back to "shipped" instead — since that's exactly the label-purchase-with-the-box-checked case, not a deliberate, later "mark complete" (which the existing fingerprint-match guard already leaves untouched, since by then the fingerprint hasn't changed since the last time this ran).

An order that already got stuck on "Completed" from this bug before the fix deployed isn't retroactively corrected — its shipment fingerprint already got recorded when it happened, so this won't re-fire for it; that one needs its status changed back to "Shipped" by hand, same as any other manual correction.

Verify: on an order in Processing or In Production, purchase a shipping label through the embedded WooCommerce Shipping form (with its own "mark order as complete" checkbox checked) → confirm the order lands on "Shipped", not "Completed" → separately, purchase a label with that checkbox unchecked → confirm it also lands on "Shipped" → later, manually change that same order's status to "Completed" from the drawer's own status dropdown → confirm that manual change is respected and not reverted.

## "Your order has shipped!" customer email (direct follow-up: "does the customer get a shipped email?" — no, and "yes, show me a mockup... Do it and deploy!")

"Shipped" is a custom status this codebase defines (`YeffoPrint_Order_Shipment_Status::STATUS`), so WooCommerce core has no built-in email for it — customers previously got a "Processing" email and then nothing until (if anything) a later status change. Two new classes, following `WC_Email_Customer_Processing_Order`'s own shape almost line for line:

- **`YeffoPrint_Email_Customer_Shipped_Order`** (`yeffoprint-core/includes/woocommerce/class-email-customer-shipped-order.php`) — a `WC_Email` subclass. Hooks `woocommerce_order_status_{$STATUS}` (`woocommerce_order_status_shipped`) — `WC_Abstract_Order::status_transition()` fires this generic `woocommerce_order_status_{$to}` action for *any* status a transition lands on, custom ones included, so no special-casing was needed to make a custom status trigger a transactional email. `trigger()` only sends when `YeffoPrint_Order_Tracking::get_shipments()` actually returns a real shipment — the same source of truth the existing "Track your order" button already checks — so a staff member manually picking "Shipped" from the dropdown with no label ever purchased doesn't send a customer an email with an empty tracking box.
- **`YeffoPrint_Order_Shipped_Email`** (`class-order-shipped-email.php`) — registers the class above into WooCommerce's own email registry via the `woocommerce_email_classes` filter, exactly how a custom `WC_Email` normally gets instantiated, sent, and given a Settings → Emails row with its own enable/subject/heading fields. `YeffoPrint_Email_Customer_Shipped_Order` itself is never `new`'d directly in the bootstrap — only this registrar is.

Two new theme templates, `yeffoprint/woocommerce/emails/customer-shipped-order.php` (html) and `.../emails/plain/customer-shipped-order.php` (plain-text) — both authored from scratch rather than overriding a WC core template, since a wholly new custom-status email has no core equivalent to fall back to. Same header/footer/order-table/customer-details structure every other customer email here already shares; the only new content is a shipping-details box (carrier + tracking number, one per shipment) reusing `class-order-item-meta.php`'s existing `.yp-email-fields`/`.yp-email-fields-box`/`.yp-email-fields-heading`/`.yp-email-fields-rows` CSS classes verbatim — the same bordered, uppercase-label box already used for per-item customization detail — so this needed no new CSS. The "Track your order" button itself needed no new code either: `class-order-tracking.php`'s `render_tracking_button()` is already hooked to `woocommerce_email_after_order_table` for every customer-facing order email and fires on this one for free the moment a shipment exists.

### Follow-up: the shipped-order email crashed the site with a critical error (direct report, immediately after the above shipped: "one of the changes you just made has crashed the site")

`YeffoPrint_Email_Customer_Shipped_Order` declares `extends \WC_Email` directly — a class-declaration dependency, not a lazy reference inside a method body, meaning the *file itself* needs `WC_Email` already resolvable the instant it's `require_once`'d. It was wired into `class-yeffoprint-core.php`'s top-level `includes()` block alongside every other class in this plugin, which runs unconditionally at plugin-file-load time — not guaranteed to be after WooCommerce's own classes are loaded. `Requires Plugins: woocommerce` (`yeffoprint-core.php`'s own plugin header) doesn't fully rule out that ordering edge case on its own, same caveat already called out in this file's existing comment above the payment-gateway classes, which hit the exact same trap earlier and already established the fix.

Moved the `require_once` for `class-email-customer-shipped-order.php` out of the eager top-level block and into `YeffoPrint_Order_Shipped_Email::register_email()` (`class-order-shipped-email.php`) — the `woocommerce_email_classes` filter callback — so the file only ever loads from deep inside WooCommerce's own fully-booted email registry, which can never fire before `WC_Email` exists. Same lazy-require pattern this plugin's `YeffoPrint_Venmo_Gateway`/`YeffoPrint_Zelle_Gateway` classes already use for the identical reason.

Verify: confirm the site loads without a critical error → confirm "Shipped order" still appears under Settings → Emails → confirm the email still sends correctly on a real shipped order.

### Follow-up: harden the shipped-order email against crashing the order-status save itself (direct report: printed a shipping label on an in-production order and its status never advanced to Shipped)

`YeffoPrint_Email_Customer_Shipped_Order::trigger()` runs synchronously inside `WC_Order::status_transition()`, itself called from `$order->save()` — the very save that had just persisted the "shipped" status to the DB. WooCommerce's own core emails only ever reach their `trigger()` via `WC_Emails::send_transactional_email()`'s own try/catch (`class-wc-emails.php`); hooking the raw `woocommerce_order_status_{status}` action directly, the way this class does (mirroring how core's own per-email classes are *dispatched*, but without going through that wrapper), bypassed that protection. A fatal anywhere in this email's own content/template code could take the whole save request down mid-flight, which could plausibly explain a shipping-label print appearing to leave an order stuck without the dashboard ever reflecting "Shipped."

Wrapped the whole body of `trigger()` in a `try`/`catch ( \Throwable $e )`, logging via `wc_get_logger()` on failure instead of letting it propagate — a bug in this one email's own code can now only cost that email, never the order save or the request it runs inside.

Verify: purchase a shipping label on an order in Processing or In Production → confirm the order lands on "Shipped" and the email sends → if a future bug does appear in this email's content code, confirm it logs to WooCommerce → Status → Logs (source: `yeffoprint-shipped-email`) instead of taking down the request.

### Follow-up: manually marking an order Shipped from the dropdown didn't send the email (direct report: "I also manually marked the order shipped and it didn't send the email")

`trigger()` originally only called `send_notification()` when `YeffoPrint_Order_Tracking::get_shipments()` found real label/tracking data on the order — deliberately, to avoid sending a "shipped" email with an empty shipping-details box when staff pick "Shipped" from the dropdown by hand with no label ever purchased through WooCommerce Shipping. That guard was too strict: a manual status change to "Shipped" is itself a real "it shipped" signal (e.g. it went out without a label purchased through this system), and the customer should still hear about it.

Removed the `get_shipments()` gate — `trigger()` now sends on any transition into "shipped", full stop. No template changes were needed: `customer-shipped-order.php` (and its plain-text counterpart) already wrap the shipping-details box in `if ( $shipments )`, so an order with no real tracking data still gets a clean "your order has shipped!" email, just without the carrier/tracking section (and without the "Track your order" button — `render_tracking_button()` already skips itself the same way when there's nothing to track).

Verify: manually change an order's status to "Shipped" from the drawer's own dropdown, on an order with no shipping label ever purchased → confirm the customer still receives "Your order has shipped!" with no shipping-details box or tracking button → separately, purchase a real label → confirm the email includes the carrier/tracking section as before.

### Follow-up: still no email on a second order that *did* have real tracking data attached (direct report, after the fix above)

Every angle checked by hand read correct: `is_enabled()`'s default (`WC_Email::init_form_fields()`'s own `'enabled' => 'yes'` default), `get_recipient()`'s `is_email()` filtering, and the hook-registration timing (this plugin's own `class-admin-order-controller.php::save_status()` changes status via a REST route, and `WC_Emails::instance()` is initialized on `rest_api_init` by WooCommerce core's own `register_wp_admin_settings()` — so the `woocommerce_order_status_shipped` hook should already be registered by the time the transition fires). None of that pointed at a concrete bug without live evidence.

Rather than guess further blind, added logging: every `trigger()` call now writes an entry to WooCommerce → Status → Logs (source `yeffoprint-shipped-email`) recording the order ID, whether the email was enabled, its resolved recipient, how many shipments `YeffoPrint_Order_Tracking::get_shipments()` found, and whether `send_notification()` reported success — win or skip, not just failure. The next occurrence should show up there with the actual runtime state instead of needing another round of speculation.

Verify: manually change an order's status to "Shipped" → confirm a new log entry appears under WooCommerce → Status → Logs with source `yeffoprint-shipped-email` showing the actual enabled/recipient/shipments/sent values for that attempt.

## Fix: root cause found — `YeffoPrint_Order_Tracking` was reading a legacy, mostly-empty WooCommerce Shipping meta key (direct report: manual "Shipped" email sent, `shipments: 0` in the log, "but there's not tracking data in it even though there is shipment tracking attached to that order in woocommerce")

The diagnostic logging above paid off immediately: it showed the shipped-order email *did* send, but `YeffoPrint_Order_Tracking::get_shipments()` found zero shipments on an order with a real, confirmed-attached label. Traced into the actual installed `woocommerce-shipping` plugin source (`class-wc-connect-service-settings-store.php`): this version has migrated label storage to a new order-meta key, `wcshipping_labels` — `get_label_order_meta_data( $order_id, $use_legacy_key = false )` reads that by default now, with the original `wc_connect_labels` key kept only for orders WC Shipping's own `LegacyLabelMigrator` hasn't converted, read only when `$use_legacy_key` is explicitly `true`. `class-order-tracking.php` had only ever read `wc_connect_labels`, so it silently found nothing on every current-generation label — which means this bug was never specific to the new shipped-order email at all. It's been silently breaking, since the day `class-order-tracking.php` shipped:

- `class-order-shipment-status.php`'s auto-advance-to-"Shipped" detection (never fired off a real label — every automatic advance the earlier "printing a shipping label" fixes were meant to restore was actually still broken)
- The "Track your order" button that's supposed to appear on *every* customer-facing order email the moment a shipment exists
- Now also the new shipped-order email's carrier/tracking box

Fixed at the source: `get_shipments()` now reads `wcshipping_labels` first, falling back to the legacy `wc_connect_labels` only if that's empty — covering both current-generation labels and any old, unmigrated order. Confirmed both meta keys share the identical entry shape (`tracking`/`carrier_id`/`refund`/etc.) directly against that plugin's own read/write code, so no other change was needed — `render_tracking_button()`, `maybe_advance_to_shipped()`, and the shipped-order email all read through this same one function and are all fixed by the same one-key change.

Verify: on an order with a real WooCommerce Shipping label already purchased, confirm `YeffoPrint_Order_Tracking::get_shipments()` now returns it (the `yeffoprint-shipped-email` log's `shipments:` count on a fresh "Shipped" transition should be > 0) → confirm the shipped-order email now shows the carrier/tracking box → confirm the "Track your order" button now appears on other customer emails (e.g. Processing) for orders with a label → separately, purchase a brand-new label going forward and confirm the order still auto-advances to "Shipped" as expected.

## Shippo integration, running alongside WooCommerce Shipping (direct request: "the woocommerce shipping plugin is awful though. Can we build something with the shippo API to replace it?" / "I'd like to run alongside it a bit")

Deliberately additive — nothing about the existing WooCommerce Shipping embed (`class-admin-order-controller.php`'s `shipping_label_available`, `app.js`'s `wcOrderShippingLabelHtml()`/`embedShippingLabel()`) changed. A second, independent panel (`shippoPanelHtml()`/`bindShippoPanel()` in `app.js`) renders right next to it in the same Pending/In Production drawer, so staff can compare before ever switching over.

- **`YeffoPrint_Shippo_Settings`** (`yeffoprint-core/includes/woocommerce/class-shippo-settings.php`) — API token storage (admin-pasted, same plain `get_option()`/`update_option()` shape as `class-stripe-webhook-secret.php`) plus a "default package" (weight + L×W×H). This store tracks no product weight/shipping-dimension data anywhere (confirmed — every WooCommerce weight/dimension field is unset on every product), so rather than build a whole per-product weight system for a first version, staff set one reusable default in Settings → Shipping and can override it per order in the drawer. Seeded to a padded 8×6 bubble mailer, 4oz — a direct answer, "not sure how much the envelope weighs," explicitly a starting estimate meant to be corrected once a real package has been weighed, not a measured value. `get_ship_from_address()` reuses WooCommerce's own Settings → General store address — direct confirmation, "the address in woocommerce settings is the from address" — rather than adding a second address to maintain.
- **`YeffoPrint_Shippo_Client`** (`class-shippo-client.php`) — thin wrapper over Shippo's REST API, same one-`call()`-helper shape as `class-telegram-client.php`. `get_rates()` creates a Shipment (`address_from` + `address_to` + one parcel, `async: false`) and returns normalized rates sorted cheapest-first — this never charges anything on the Shippo account. `purchase_label()` creates a Transaction against a chosen rate's `object_id` — this is a real charge, and is the only call in this whole feature that is.
- **`YeffoPrint_Admin_Shippo_Controller`** (`includes/rest/admin/class-admin-shippo-controller.php`) — `POST /admin/order/{id}/shippo/rates` and `.../shippo/purchase`. Address-to is built server-side from the order's own shipping address (falling back to billing, same rule `detail_payload()`'s own `shipping_address` field already uses) — the frontend only ever submits package dimensions, never re-submits customer address data it already has. A successful purchase calls `YeffoPrint_Order_Tracking::record_shippo_label()` then `$order->save()` — the exact same save `class-order-shipment-status.php`'s `maybe_advance_to_shipped()` is already hooked to, so an order still in Processing/In Production auto-advances to Shipped and the shipped-order email fires, identically to a WooCommerce Shipping label, with zero changes to either of those.
- **`class-order-tracking.php`** — `get_shipments()` now also reads a third meta key, `yeffoprint_shippo_labels` (`record_shippo_label()`'s own write side), merged in before the existing per-entry loop. Deliberately written in the exact same shape (`tracking`/`carrier_id`/`refund`) as WC Shipping's own labels, so every downstream reader — the "Track your order" button, the auto-advance logic, the shipped-order email — picks up a Shippo-purchased label with no special-casing anywhere else.
- **Settings → Shipping** gets a new "Shippo (Beta)" panel: API token field + the four default-package number fields, wired straight through `class-admin-settings-controller.php`'s existing single GET/POST pair — no classic Settings-API (`register_setting()`/`add_settings_field()`) registration was added for these fields, since that page is already hidden from the admin menu (Phase 8) and reachable by no UI the REST controller's own `update_option()` calls ever go through.
- **Drawer UI** (`app.js`): weight/dimension inputs pre-filled from the default package → "Get Rates" → a plain rate table (carrier/service/days/price, cheapest first) → "Purchase Selected Label", gated behind a native `confirm()` stating the exact carrier/service/price before it fires. On success, shows the tracking number and a "Print label" link, and updates the drawer's own status `<select>` in place if the order auto-advanced to Shipped.

Live tracking-status pull (Shippo's Track API) into the dashboard's existing Shipped Packages panel is a deliberate follow-up, not part of this change.

Verify: paste a Shippo API token into Settings → Shipping, save → open an order in the drawer, confirm a "Shippo (Beta)" panel now appears next to the existing WooCommerce Shipping one → adjust the default package if needed, click Get Rates, confirm real rates come back cheapest-first with no charge → select one, click Purchase, confirm the browser's own confirmation dialog states the exact price before anything fires → confirm the label purchases, the tracking number/print link show, and (if the order was Processing/In Production) its status auto-advances to Shipped with the shipped-order email sending, same as a WooCommerce Shipping label would.

## Fix: cart quantity read as "1" instead of "10" (direct report, with a screenshot)

`global.css`'s blanket form-field rule (`input:not([type="checkbox"])...{ width: 100%; padding: 0.75rem 1rem; font-size: --medium }`, meant for real text fields like login/checkout inputs) was also landing on WooCommerce Blocks' own small, fixed-width cart quantity `<input type="number">` — 1rem of left+right padding at medium size left no room for a second digit in that narrow stepper box, so a quantity of 10 clipped down to showing just "1".

Added a targeted override for `.wc-block-components-quantity-selector__input` (`woocommerce.css`, same file as this theme's other WC Blocks-specific overrides) — fixed 3rem width instead of 100%, smaller padding/font-size, centered text — sized to comfortably fit two digits instead of inheriting the blanket rule meant for full-width fields.

Verify: add a line item to the cart with quantity ≥ 10 → confirm the full two-digit number is visible in the quantity box, not clipped.
